\liminf_{t\to+\infty}\|(U_{t}+U_{-t})X\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},\Delta_{\ell,a,t})}=0

for some
\ell>0
. Again, consider the function
f=\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}}X
and denote
g=e^{-i(a-\ell)z}f
. As in the proof of Theorem
2.9
, we have

\liminf_{t\to+\infty}\left(\inf_{u\in\mathcal{B}_{r}}\|e^{itx}f-(u-e^{-itx}f)\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\right)=0.

That gives (
2.30
) and the rest of the argument repeats the proof of the Theorem
2.9
.
∎

2.4.
Long-time asymptotics of the evolution in Szegő case: preliminaries

In the rest of the section, we are going to study the long-time behavior of the group

U_{t}=e^{it\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{H}}}
. To this end, we need to do some additional work first.
In this subsection, we collect all necessary definitions and auxiliary results. In many places, the presentation follows
[
6
]
,
[
7
]
and
[
9
]
, where one can find more details and references. Let
\mathcal{H}
be a singular Hamiltonian on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
and let
\Phi=\Bigl(\!\begin{smallmatrix}\Phi^{+}\\
\Phi^{-}\\
\end{smallmatrix}\!\Bigr)
be the solution of the Cauchy problem
J\Phi^{\prime}(\tau,z)=z\mathcal{H}\Phi(\tau,z)
,
\Phi(0,z)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
1\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)
,
\tau\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
z\in\mathbb{C}
. Recall that
\Theta=\Bigl(\!\begin{smallmatrix}\Theta^{+}\\
\Theta^{-}\\
\end{smallmatrix}\!\Bigr)
solves the same differential equation but satisfies different boundary condition:
\Theta(0,z)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. The Titchmarsh-Weyl function of any singular Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}
, which is not equal to
\kappa\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0\\
0&1\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)
a.e. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
for some function
\kappa
, is defined by

(2.39)

m(z)=\lim_{\tau\to+\infty}\frac{\Phi^{-}(\tau,z)}{\Theta^{-}(\tau,z)},\qquad z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}.

That function is analytic and takes
\mathbb{C}_{+}
into its closure
\overline{\mathbb{C}_{+}}
. The Herglotz representation of
m
has the form

(2.40)

m(z)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\frac{1}{x-z}-\frac{x}{x^{2}+1}\right)d\mu(x)+b_{0}z+a_{0},\quad z\in\mathbb{C}_{+},

where
\mu
is called the main spectral measure of
\mathcal{H}
,
b_{0}\geqslant 0
, and
a_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. Given proper
\mathcal{H}
, define
\mathcal{H}_{r}
by
\tau\mapsto\mathcal{H}(\tau+r)
for every
r>0
. Let
m_{r}
,
\mu_{r}
,
b_{r}
,
a_{r}
denote the Titchmarsh-Weyl function of
\mathcal{H}_{r}
, its spectral measure, and the coefficients in the Herglotz representation (
2.40
) for
m_{r}
. Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{H}}(r)

\displaystyle=\mathop{\rm Im}m_{r}(i)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{d\mu_{r}(x)}{x^{2}+1}+b_{r},

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{H}}(r)

\displaystyle=\mathop{\rm Re}m_{r}(i)=a_{r},

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{H}}(r)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\log w_{r}(x)}{x^{2}+1}\,dx,

where
\mu_{r}=w_{r}\,dx+\mu_{r,\mathbf{s}}
. It is well-known that
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{H}}
is identically zero if the Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}
is diagonal (see, e.g., Lemma 2.2 in
[
6
]
). The quantity
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{H}}(r)=\log\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{H}}(r)-\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{H}}(r)
,
r\geqslant 0
is called the entropy function of
\mathcal{H}
.
Jensen’s inequality gives
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{H}}(r)\geqslant 0
for all
r\geqslant 0
and we have
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{H}}(0)<\infty
if and only if
\mu\in\mathrm{Sz}(\mathbb{R})
. In
[
9
]
, it was proved that
\mu\in\mathrm{Sz}(\mathbb{R})
implies
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{H}}(r)<\infty
for every
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Moreover, the function
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{H}}
is absolutely continuous, non-increasing,
\lim_{r\to\infty}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{H}}=0
, and

(2.41)

\|\mathcal{K}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{H}}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}=\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{H}}(0),

see Lemma 2.3 and Lemma 2.4 in
[
9
]
.
We will need an auxiliary matrix-function
G

(2.42)

G:=\begin{pmatrix}1/\sqrt{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{H}}}&\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{H}}/\sqrt{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{H}}}\\
0&\sqrt{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{H}}}\end{pmatrix},\qquad G^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}\sqrt{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{H}}}&-\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{H}}/\sqrt{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{H}}}\\
0&1/\sqrt{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{H}}}\end{pmatrix}.

For
\mathcal{H}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}h_{1}&h\\
h&h_{2}\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, the formula

(G^{-1})^{*}\mathcal{H}G^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{H}}h_{1}&-\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{H}}h_{1}+h\\
-\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{H}}h_{1}+h&(\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}h_{1}-2\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{H}}h+h_{2})/\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{H}}\end{pmatrix}

holds.
It was proved in Lemma 2.4 of
[
9
]
, that for every
\mathcal{H}
whose spectral measure is in the Szegő class
\mathrm{Sz}(\mathbb{R})
, the function
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{H}}
satisfies

(2.43)

\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}=2\sqrt{\det\mathcal{H}}-\mathop{\mathrm{trace}}\nolimits(G^{-1})^{*}\mathcal{H}G^{-1}.

Recall that
\Theta=\Theta(\tau,z)
is the solution of the Cauchy problem (
2.5
). Define

\widetilde{\Theta}(\tau,z)=G(\tau)\Theta(\tau,z)
,

\tilde{E}_{\tau}(z)=\widetilde{\Theta}^{+}(\tau,z)+i\widetilde{\Theta}^{-}(\tau,z)
, and
\tilde{E}^{\sharp}_{\tau}(z)=\widetilde{\Theta}^{+}(\tau,z)-i\widetilde{\Theta}^{-}(\tau,z)
.

Lemma 2.14
.

We have

E_{\tau}(z)\overline{E_{\tau}(\lambda)}-E_{\tau}^{\sharp}(z)\overline{E_{\tau}^{\sharp}(\lambda)}=\tilde{E}_{\tau}(z)\overline{\tilde{E}_{\tau}(\lambda)}-\tilde{E}_{\tau}^{\sharp}(z)\overline{\tilde{E}_{\tau}^{\sharp}(\lambda)}

for all
z,\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
,
\tau\geqslant 0
.

Proof.
Take
z,\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
,
\tau\geqslant 0
. We have

\displaystyle\tilde{E}_{\tau}(z)\overline{\tilde{E}_{\tau}(\lambda)}-\tilde{E}_{\tau}^{\sharp}(z)\overline{\tilde{E}_{\tau}^{\sharp}(\lambda)}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{E}_{\tau}(z)\\
\tilde{E}_{\tau}^{\sharp}(z)\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{E}_{\tau}(\lambda)\\
\tilde{E}_{\tau}^{\sharp}(\lambda)\end{pmatrix}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}

\displaystyle=-2i\left\langle J\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{\Theta}^{+}(\tau,z)\\
\widetilde{\Theta}^{-}(\tau,z)\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{\Theta}^{+}(\tau,\lambda)\\
\widetilde{\Theta}^{-}(\tau,\lambda)\end{pmatrix}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}

\displaystyle=-2i\left\langle G^{*}(\tau)JG(\tau)\begin{pmatrix}\Theta^{+}(\tau,z)\\
\Theta^{-}(\tau,z)\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\Theta^{+}(\tau,\lambda)\\
\Theta^{-}(\tau,\lambda)\end{pmatrix}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}.

For every
\tau\geqslant 0
, the matrix
G(\tau)
has real entries and unit determinant which gives
G^{*}(\tau)JG(\tau)=J
. Thus, we have

\displaystyle\tilde{E}_{\tau}(z)\overline{\tilde{E}_{\tau}(\lambda)}-\tilde{E}_{\tau}^{\sharp}(z)\overline{\tilde{E}_{\tau}^{\sharp}(\lambda)}

\displaystyle=-2i\left\langle J\begin{pmatrix}\Theta^{+}(\tau,z)\\
\Theta^{-}(\tau,z)\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\Theta^{+}(\tau,\lambda)\\
\Theta^{-}(\tau,\lambda)\end{pmatrix}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}=E_{\tau}(z)\overline{E_{\tau}(\lambda)}-E_{\tau}^{\sharp}(z)\overline{E_{\tau}^{\sharp}(\lambda)}

and that proves the statement. ∎

Since
E_{\tau}
is Hermite-Biehler function, taking
z=\lambda
in the last lemma implies that
\tilde{E}_{\tau}
is Hermite-Biehler function as well, and hence it has no zeroes in
\mathbb{C}_{+}
.
Define
\alpha_{\tau}\in\mathbb{T}
such that
\alpha_{\tau}\tilde{E}_{\tau}(i)>0
and put

(2.44)

\widetilde{P}_{2\tau}(z)=\bar{\alpha}_{\tau}e^{iT_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau)z}\tilde{E}^{\sharp}_{\tau}(z),\qquad\widetilde{P}_{2\tau}^{*}(z)=\alpha_{\tau}e^{iT_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau)z}\tilde{E}_{\tau}(z),\qquad z\in\mathbb{C},\quad\tau\geqslant 0.

As in Section 4 of
[
9
]
, we call
\widetilde{P}_{\tau}
and
\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}
the regularized Krein’s orthogonal entire functions generated by
\mu
. To some extent, the introduction of these functions will allow us to use ideas of the theory of polynomials orthogonal on the unit circle, see, e.g., Lemma
2.16
below. Both
\Phi^{+},\Phi^{-},\Theta^{+}
, and
\Theta^{-}
are entire functions of finite exponential type (see, e.g., Lemma 17 in
[
73
]
) so
\widetilde{P}_{2\tau}
and
\widetilde{P}_{2\tau}^{*}
have finite exponential type as well.
Their basic properties were studied in the papers
[
7
]
,
[
9
]
and we discuss some of them now.
From the definition, it is immediate that
\widetilde{P}_{2\tau}^{*}
satisfies relation
|\widetilde{P}_{2\tau}^{*}(x)|=|\tilde{E}_{\tau}(x)|
for
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Therefore, by Theorem 1.3 in
[
9
]
we have

(2.45)

\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{|\log|\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}(x)|^{-2}-\log w(x)|}{x^{2}+1}\,dx=0.

The formula (
2.17
) and the Lemma
2.14
yield

(2.46)

k_{\mathcal{B}_{\tau},\lambda}(z)=-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\frac{\tilde{E}_{\tau}(z)\overline{\tilde{E}_{\tau}(\lambda)}-\tilde{E}_{\tau}^{\sharp}(z)\overline{\tilde{E}_{\tau}^{\sharp}(\lambda)}}{z-\bar{\lambda}},\qquad z\in\mathbb{C}.

Consider now the “shifted” Hilbert space
e^{iT_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau)z}\mathcal{B}_{\tau}
. From (
2.46
), we conclude that its reproducing kernel at
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
is given by

(2.47)

\displaystyle K_{\tau,\lambda}(z)

\displaystyle=-\frac{e^{iT_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau)(z-\bar{\lambda})}}{2\pi i}\frac{\tilde{E}_{\tau}(z)\overline{\tilde{E}_{\tau}(\lambda)}-\tilde{E}_{\tau}^{\sharp}(z)\overline{\tilde{E}_{\tau}^{\sharp}(\lambda)}}{z-\bar{\lambda}},

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\frac{\widetilde{P}_{2\tau}^{*}(z)\overline{\widetilde{P}_{2\tau}^{*}(\lambda)}-\widetilde{P}_{2\tau}(z)\overline{\widetilde{P}_{2\tau}(\lambda)}}{z-\bar{\lambda}}.

In the case when
\det\mathcal{H}=1
, the following result was obtained in Lemma 4.1 of
[
9
]
where the expression for
\alpha_{\tau}
was found in terms of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{H}}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{H}}
.

Lemma 2.15
.

For every
\tau>0
,
the function
\widetilde{P}_{2\tau}^{*}
is outer in
\mathbb{C}_{+}
.

Proof.
Recall that
\tilde{E}_{\tau}
is a Hermite-Biehler function. By definition, it can be written as

(2.48)

\displaystyle\tilde{E}_{\tau}=\frac{E_{\tau}(\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{H}}+i(\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{H}}+1))-E_{\tau}^{\sharp}(\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{H}}+i(\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{H}}-1))}{2i\sqrt{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{H}}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{E_{\tau}}{2i\sqrt{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{H}}}}\Bigl(\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{H}}+i(\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{H}}+1)\Bigr)\Bigl(1-\frac{E^{\sharp}_{\tau}}{E_{\tau}}\frac{\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{H}}+i(\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{H}}-1)}{\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{H}}+i(\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{H}}+1)}\Bigr).

The formula (
2.21
) says

\mathop{type}\nolimits E_{\tau}=\limsup_{y\to+\infty}\frac{\log|E_{\tau}(iy)|}{y}=T_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau).

Since
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{H}}>0
and
|E^{\sharp}_{\tau}/E_{\tau}|<1
in
\mathbb{C}_{+}
, we get
|(\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{H}}+i(\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{H}}-1))/(\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{H}}+i(\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{H}}+1))|<1
and

(2.49)

\limsup_{y\to+\infty}\frac{\log|\tilde{E}_{\tau}(iy)|}{y}=T_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau).

The formula (
2.48
) shows that
\tilde{E}_{\tau}
is a linear combination of
E_{\tau}
and
E^{\sharp}_{\tau}
, two functions of exponential type
T_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau)
, and so its exponential type is at most
T_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau)
. Thus, identity (
2.49
) gives
\mathop{type}\nolimits\tilde{E}_{\tau}=T_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau)
.

Recall again that
\tilde{E}_{\tau}
is Hermite-Biehler function. If it has no roots in
\mathbb{C}_{-}
, then
\tilde{E}_{\tau}=Ce^{-iT_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau)z}
with some nonzero constant
C
. Hence,
\widetilde{P}_{2\tau}^{*}
is a positive constant and we are done. If
\tilde{E}_{\tau}
does have a root in
\mathbb{C}_{-}
, we call it
\lambda
and argue as follows. Since
\tilde{E}_{\tau}(\lambda)=0
, we also have
\widetilde{P}_{2\tau}(\bar{\lambda})=0
by definition. Formula (
2.47
) takes the form

(2.50)

K_{\tau,\bar{\lambda}}(z)=-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\frac{\widetilde{P}_{2\tau}^{*}(z)\overline{\widetilde{P}_{2\tau}^{*}(\bar{\lambda})}}{z-\lambda}.

Function
K_{\tau,\bar{\lambda}}(z)
is a reproducing kernel of
e^{iT_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau)z}\mathcal{B}_{\tau}
at point
\bar{\lambda}
. Thus,
K_{\tau,\bar{\lambda}}
belongs to this space and, by (
2.12
),

K_{\tau,\bar{\lambda}}=e^{iT_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau)z}E_{\tau}g
with some
g\in H_{2}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
. The function
E_{\tau}
is of bounded type in
\mathbb{C}_{+}
. Hence,
K_{\tau,\bar{\lambda}}
is also of bounded type there. We know that
\widetilde{P}_{2\tau}^{*}
is entire and has no roots in
\mathbb{C}_{+}
because
\tilde{E}_{\tau}
is Hermite-Biehler and has no roots there.
Hence, Smirnov-Nevanlinna factorization of
K_{\tau,\bar{\lambda}}
can be written as

K_{\tau,\bar{\lambda}}=\xi e^{icz}\mathcal{O},

where
\xi\in\mathbb{T}
is a constant,
c\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\mathcal{O}
is outer. Since

\lim_{y\to+\infty}\frac{\log|\mathcal{O}(iy)|}{y}=0,

we also have

-c=\limsup_{y\to+\infty}\frac{\log|K_{\tau,\bar{\lambda}}(iy)|}{y}=\limsup_{y\to+\infty}\frac{\log|\widetilde{P}_{2\tau}^{*}(iy)|}{y}=0,

where (
2.49
), (
2.50
), and the definition of
\widetilde{P}_{2\tau}^{*}
have been used. Since
c=0
, we get
K_{\tau,\bar{\lambda}}=\xi\mathcal{O}
and the formula (
2.50
) along with normalization
\widetilde{P}_{2\tau}^{*}(i)>0
prove the lemma.
∎

Given a measure
\mu=w\,dx+\mu_{\mathbf{s}}
in
\mathrm{Sz}(\mathbb{R})
, we denote by
D_{\mu}
its Szegő function:

(2.51)

D_{\mu}(z)=\exp\left(\frac{1}{\pi i}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\log\sqrt{w(x)}\left(\frac{1}{x-z}-\frac{x}{x^{2}+1}\right)dx\right),\qquad z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}.

In other words,
D_{\mu}
is the outer function in
\mathbb{C}_{+}
such that
D_{\mu}(i)>0
and
|D_{\mu}|^{2}=w
almost everywhere on the real line
\mathbb{R}
in the sense of non-tangential boundary values.

The following lemma will play a key role later on.

Lemma 2.16
.

Let
\mu=w\,dx+\mu_{\mathbf{s}}
be a measure in
\mathrm{Sz}(\mathbb{R})
, and let
\widetilde{P}_{\tau}
,
\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}
be its regularized Krein’s orthogonal entire functions. Then,

(2.52)

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}(z)=D_{\mu}^{-1}(z),

(2.53)

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\widetilde{P}_{\tau}(z)=0,

uniformly on compacts in
\mathbb{C}_{+}
, and

(2.54)

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{|\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}(x)|^{2}}{x^{2}+1}d\mu_{\mathbf{s}}(x)=0,

(2.55)

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{|\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}(x)-D^{-1}_{\mu}(x)|^{2}}{x^{2}+1}w(x)\,dx=0.

Proof.
Formula (
2.45
) gives (
2.52
) after comparing the multiplicative representations for outer functions
\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}
and
D_{\mu}^{-1}
. Let
\mathcal{B}_{\tau}
be defined by (
2.10
). The standard variational property of the reproducing kernel yields

(2.56)

{\|K_{\tau,\lambda}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=\sup\{|f(\lambda)|\colon f\in e^{iT_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau)z}\mathcal{B}_{\tau},\;\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leqslant 1\}.}

We claim that
\|K_{\tau,\lambda}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}
is non-decreasing in
\tau\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. To prove it, we first notice that the space
e^{ibz}\mathcal{B}_{L_{\mathcal{H}}(b)}
coincides with the completion in
L^{2}(\mu)
of the set
e^{ibz}\mathcal{E}_{b}
for every fixed
b>0
according to Theorem
2.5
. It follows that

(2.57)

\|K_{L_{\mathcal{H}}(b),\lambda}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=\sup\{|f(\lambda)|\colon f\in e^{ibz}\mathcal{E}_{b},\;\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leqslant 1\}.

Since
e^{ib_{1}z}\mathcal{E}_{b_{1}}\subseteq e^{ib_{2}z}\mathcal{E}_{b_{2}}
if
b_{1}\leqslant b_{2}
, we have

(2.58)

\|K_{L_{\mathcal{H}}(b_{1}),\lambda}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leqslant\|K_{L_{\mathcal{H}}(b_{2}),\lambda}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)},

provided that
0<b_{1}\leqslant b_{2}
.
Now, take arbitrary positive
\tau>0
and let
b=T_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau)
. We have

\mathcal{B}_{\tau}\subseteq\bigcap_{\varepsilon>0}\mathcal{B}_{L_{\mathcal{H}}(b+\varepsilon)}\subseteq\bigcap_{\varepsilon>0}\mathop{\mathrm{clos}}\nolimits_{L^{2}(\mu)}\mathcal{E}_{b+\varepsilon}
with the last inclusion
following from Theorem
2.5
.
Hence,
e^{iT_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau)z}\mathcal{B}_{\tau}\subseteq\bigcap_{\varepsilon>0}\mathop{\mathrm{clos}}\nolimits_{L^{2}(\mu)}(e^{i(b+\varepsilon)z}\mathcal{E}_{b+\varepsilon})
.
From (
2.56
), (
2.57
), and (
2.58
), we get
\|K_{\tau,\lambda}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leqslant\inf_{\varepsilon>0}\|K_{L_{\mathcal{H}}(b+\varepsilon),\lambda}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}
. Finally, if we have
\tau_{1}<\tau_{2}
for which
T_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau_{1})=T_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau_{2})=b
, then
\mathcal{B}_{\tau_{1}}\subsetneq\mathcal{B}_{\tau_{2}}
and (
2.56
) yields

\|K_{\tau_{1},\lambda}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leqslant\|K_{\tau_{2},\lambda}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

in that situation too. Putting together all cases, we get our claim.
Therefore,
\|K_{\tau,\lambda}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}
is non-decreasing in
\tau\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. In particular, we have

\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\|K_{\tau,\lambda}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=\lim_{\eta\to\infty}\|K_{L_{\mathcal{H}}(\eta),\lambda}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

By the Krein-Wiener theorem (combine formulas
(9.9)
,
(9.13)
, and
(9.14)
in
[
24
]
), we have

\lim_{b\to\infty}\|K_{L_{\mathcal{H}}(b),\lambda}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}=\frac{1}{4\pi}\frac{|D_{\mu}(\lambda)|^{-2}}{\mathop{\rm Im}\lambda},

where the convergence is uniform on compact sets in
\mathbb{C}_{+}
. Since
K_{\tau,\lambda}
is a reproducing kernel, one has

(2.59)

\|K_{\tau,\lambda}(\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}=K_{\tau,\lambda}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{4\pi}\frac{|\widetilde{P}_{2\tau}^{*}(\lambda)|^{2}-|\widetilde{P}_{2\tau}(\lambda)|^{2}}{\mathop{\rm Im}\lambda}

for every
\tau\geqslant 0
. It follows that

(2.60)

\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\frac{1}{4\pi}\frac{|\widetilde{P}_{2\tau}^{*}(\lambda)|^{2}-|\widetilde{P}_{2\tau}(\lambda)|^{2}}{\mathop{\rm Im}\lambda}=\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\|K_{\tau,\lambda}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}=\lim_{b\to\infty}\|K_{L_{\mathcal{H}}(b),\lambda}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}=\frac{1}{4\pi}\frac{|D_{\mu}(\lambda)|^{-2}}{\mathop{\rm Im}\lambda}

holds locally uniformly in
\mathbb{C}_{+}
.
Combined with (
2.52
), that implies (
2.53
). From (
2.45
) and Jensen’s inequality, we get

(2.61)

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\log(|\widetilde{P}^{*}_{\tau}(x)|^{2}w(x))}{x^{2}+1}\,dx\right)\leqslant\limsup_{\tau\to\infty}\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{|\widetilde{P}^{*}_{\tau}(x)|^{2}}{x^{2}+1}\,d\mu(x).

On the other hand, one can write

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{\widetilde{P}^{*}_{\tau}}{x+i}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}(i)|}\left\|\frac{\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}\overline{\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}(i)}}{x+i}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{|\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}(i)|}\left\|\frac{\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}\overline{\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}(i)}-\widetilde{P}_{\tau}\overline{\widetilde{P}_{\tau}(i)}}{x+i}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}+\left|\frac{\widetilde{P}_{\tau}(i)}{\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}(i)}\right|\cdot\left\|\frac{\widetilde{P}_{\tau}}{x+i}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

(2.62)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}(i)|}\left\|\frac{\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}\overline{\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}(i)}-\widetilde{P}_{\tau}\overline{\widetilde{P}_{\tau}(i)}}{x+i}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}+\left|\frac{\widetilde{P}_{\tau}(i)}{\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}(i)}\right|\cdot\left\|\frac{\widetilde{P}^{*}_{\tau}}{x+i}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Relations (
2.52
) and (
2.53
) yield
\lim_{\tau\to+\infty}|\widetilde{P}_{\tau}(i)/\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}(i)|=0
. We also have

\displaystyle\limsup_{\tau\to\infty}\frac{1}{|\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}(i)|^{2}}\left\|\frac{\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}\overline{\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}(i)}-\widetilde{P}_{\tau}\overline{\widetilde{P}_{\tau}(i)}}{x+i}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{fi1}}}{{=}}\limsup_{\tau\to\infty}\frac{4\pi^{2}}{|\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}(i)|^{2}}\|K_{\tau/2,i}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq67}}}{{=}}\pi\limsup_{\tau\to\infty}\frac{|\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}(i)|^{2}-|\widetilde{P}_{\tau}(i)|^{2}}{|\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}(i)|^{2}}=\pi.

That identity, along with (
2.62
), yields
\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}/(x+i)\in L^{2}(\mu)
and
\limsup_{\tau\to\infty}\|\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}/(x+i)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leqslant\pi
. Moreover, the inequality in (
2.61
) is, in fact, equality, and we get

(2.63)

\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{|\widetilde{P}^{*}_{\tau}(x)|^{2}}{x^{2}+1}\,d\mu(x)=1.

Next, we claim that

(2.64)

\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}(x)D_{\mu}(x)}{x^{2}+1}\,dx=\widetilde{P}^{*}_{\tau}(i)D_{\mu}(i).

Given the properties of
\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}
, this is nearly obvious. However, in the next few lines, we give the proof of (
2.64
).
Indeed, as showed in Lemma
2.15
above,
\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}
is outer in
\mathbb{C}_{+}
. Then, the function
\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}D_{\mu}/(z+i)
lies in
N_{+}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
and has non-tangential boundary values in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
thanks to the following bound

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|\frac{\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}(x)D_{\mu}(x)}{x+i}\right|^{2}\,dx\leqslant\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{|\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}(x)|^{2}}{x^{2}+1}\,d\mu(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{sa2}}}{{<}}\infty.

Hence,
\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}D_{\mu}/(z+i)
is in
N_{+}(\mathbb{C}_{+})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R})=H^{2}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
(see the discussion after Theorem 5.4 in
[
33
]
concerning the last equality of sets). Therefore, the function
f
defined by

f(\xi)=\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}(z)D_{\mu}(z),\qquad z\in\mathbb{C}_{+},\qquad\xi=\frac{i-z}{i+z}\in\mathbb{D}

belongs to the Hardy space
H^{2}(\mathbb{D})
in the open unit disk
\mathbb{D}
as established in Chapter VI.C in
[
49
]
. The mean-value formula for functions in
H^{2}(\mathbb{D})
yields

\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}f(e^{i\theta})\,d\theta=f(0).

That gives (
2.64
), when written in terms of
z
.

Having proved (
2.64
), we can write

(2.65)

\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{|\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}(x)-D^{-1}_{\mu}(x)|^{2}}{x^{2}+1}|D_{\mu}(x)|^{2}\,dx=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{|\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}(x)D_{\mu}(x)|^{2}}{x^{2}+1}\,dx+1-2\mathop{\rm Re}(\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}(i)D_{\mu}(i)).

Notice now that

\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{|\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}(x)D_{\mu}(x)|^{2}}{x^{2}+1}\,dx+\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{|\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}(x)|^{2}}{x^{2}+1}\,d\mu_{\mathbf{s}}(x)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{|\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}(x)|^{2}}{x^{2}+1}\,d\mu(x)

and the right-hand side converges to
1
when
\tau\to\infty
by (
2.63
). Then,

\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\left(1-2\mathop{\rm Re}(\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}(i)D_{\mu}(i))\right)=-1

by (
2.52
). Thus, (
2.65
) yields

0\leqslant\limsup_{\tau\to\infty}\left(\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{|\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}(x)-D^{-1}_{\mu}(x)|^{2}}{x^{2}+1}|D_{\mu}(x)|^{2}\,dx+\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{|\widetilde{P}_{\tau}^{*}(x)|^{2}}{x^{2}+1}\,d\mu_{\mathbf{s}}(x)\right)=0.

Therefore, (
2.54
) and (
2.55
) follow. ∎

The Lemmas
2.17
and
2.18
below are not new. We give their proofs for the reader’s convenience.

Lemma 2.17
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a proper Hamiltonian on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Then, the set
\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{H}}\cap H_{c}
is dense in
H
.

Proof.
Consider the linear manifold of functions
X\in H
for which there is
L\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\setminus\cup_{I\in\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{H})}I
such that
X
can be written as follows

X(\tau)=\begin{cases}J\int_{\tau}^{L}\mathcal{H}(s)Y(s)\,ds,&\tau\leqslant L\\
0,&\tau>L\end{cases},\qquad Y\in H:\quad\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\nolimits Y\subseteq[0,L],\quad(Y,\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right))_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}=0.

In the proof of Theorem 3 in
[
73
]
, it was showed that if
X_{0}\in H
is orthogonal to that linear manifold, then
\mathcal{H}X_{0}=0
almost everywhere on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
(we apply Theorem 3 of
[
73
]
to Hamiltonians
\mathcal{H}
that do not coincide with those that are equal to
\kappa\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0\\
0&1\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)
on some interval
[0,\varepsilon]
, since we study only such Hamiltonians in our paper). That implies this manifold is dense in
H
. On the other hand, every
X
in that manifold has compact support and

\langle X(0),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
1\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}=\langle JX(0),J\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
1\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}=-(Y,\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right))_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}=0.

Therefore,
X\in\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{H}}\cap H_{c}
. The lemma follows. ∎

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a proper Hamiltonian on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, and let
H
be the Hilbert space generated by
\mathcal{H}
. On functions
X\in L_{c}^{2}(\mathcal{H})
, define

(2.66)

\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{H}}\colon X\mapsto\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\langle\mathcal{H}(\tau)X(\tau),\Theta(\tau,\bar{z})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}\,d\tau,\qquad z\in\mathbb{C}.

Note that
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{H}}
coincides with
\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}}
on
H
. Denote by
P_{H}
the orthogonal projector in
L^{2}(\mathcal{H})
to
H
. The orthogonal complement
L^{2}(\mathcal{H})\ominus H
consists of functions
X
that satisfy the following conditions:

X=0
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\setminus\bigcup_{I\in\mathfrak{I}(\mathcal{H})}I
and

\quad\int_{I}\kappa(\tau)\langle X(\tau),e_{I}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}\,d\tau=0,\qquad\mathcal{H}(\tau)=\kappa(\tau)\langle\cdot,e_{I}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}e_{I},\qquad\tau\in I,\qquad I\in\mathfrak{I}(\mathcal{H}),

where
e_{I}\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\|e_{I}\|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}=1
, and
\kappa>0
a.e. on each
I
.
It follows that
P_{H}\colon L^{2}(\mathcal{H})\to H
coincides with the operator

X\mapsto\begin{cases}X(\tau),&\tau\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\setminus\bigcup_{I\in\mathfrak{I}(\mathcal{H})}I,\\
\sum_{I\in\mathfrak{I}(\mathcal{H})}c_{I}\chi_{I}e_{I},&\tau\in\bigcup_{I\in\mathfrak{I}(\mathcal{H})}I,\end{cases}\qquad c_{I}=\frac{\int_{I}\kappa(\tau)\langle X(\tau),e_{I}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}\,d\tau}{\int_{I}\kappa(\tau)\,d\tau}.

Indeed, this operator is linear, vanishes on
L^{2}(\mathcal{H})\ominus H
, and acts as an identity on
H
because
\chi_{I}(e_{I}+e_{I}^{\bot})=\chi_{I}e_{I}
in
L^{2}(\mathcal{H})
for every vector
e_{I}^{\bot}\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
orthogonal to
e_{I}
. As a consequence, if
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\setminus\bigcup_{I\in\mathfrak{I}(\mathcal{H})}I
and
\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\nolimits X\subseteq[0,r]
, then
\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\nolimits P_{H}X\subseteq[0,r]
. We use this observation in the formula (
2.67
) below.

Lemma 2.18
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a proper Hamiltonian on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
and let
\mu
be its spectral measure. We have
\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{H}}=P_{H}
and
\|\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{H}}X\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leqslant\|X\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}
for every
X\in L^{2}(\mathcal{H})
.

Proof.
Consider
X\in L^{2}(\mathcal{H})
such that
\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\nolimits X\subseteq[0,r]
, where
r
is not in the interior of an indivisible interval (that is,
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\setminus\bigcup_{I\in\mathfrak{I}(\mathcal{H})}I)
. Then, taking any

z\in\mathbb{C}
, we have

(\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{H}}X)(z)=(X,\Theta(\cdot,z))_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}=(X,\chi_{[0,r]}\Theta(\cdot,z))_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}.

If
I
is an indivisible interval, we have
\mathcal{H}(\tau)=\kappa(\tau)\langle\cdot,e_{I}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}e_{I}
with some vector
e_{I}\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\,\|e_{I}\|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}=1
for
\tau\in I
. Equation

J\Theta^{\prime}(\tau,z)=z\kappa(\tau)\langle\Theta(\tau,z),e_{I}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}e_{I},\,\tau\in I

implies
\Theta^{\prime}(\tau,z)=-z\kappa(\tau)\langle\Theta(\tau,z),e_{I}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}Je_{I},

\tau\in I\,
. Since
\langle Je_{I},e_{I}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}=0
, we have
\langle\Theta^{\prime}(\tau,z),e_{I}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}=0
, and hence
\langle\Theta(\tau,z),e_{I}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}
is constant in
\tau
on
I
.
That gives
\chi_{[0,r]}\Theta(\cdot,z)\in H
for every
z
in a sense that
\Theta(\tau,z)
is constant on each
I
when considered as an element of
L^{2}(\mathcal{H})
defined in (
2.2
). Thus, we have

(2.67)

(X,\chi_{[0,r]}\Theta(\cdot,z))_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}=(P_{H}X,\chi_{[0,r]}\Theta(\cdot,z))_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}=(\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}}P_{H}X)(z).

That gives
\|\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{H}}X\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leqslant\|X\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}
and
\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{H}}X=P_{H}X
. The set of
X
we considered is dense in
L^{2}(\mathcal{H})
and the operator
\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}}
is unitary. Therefore, the lemma is true for all
X\in L^{2}(\mathcal{H})
.∎

2.5.
Long-time asymptotics of the evolution in Szegő case: the main result

Recall that we study the evolution
U_{t}X
when
X\in H
and
t\to\pm\infty
. We will describe
U_{t}X
in terms of the “free” evolution
U^{0}_{t}Y_{X,\pm}
of some states
Y_{X,\pm}\in L^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{0})
as
t\to\pm\infty
, where
U_{t}^{0}
is generated by “free” Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}_{0}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Note that
U_{t}
,
U^{0}_{t}
act on different Hilbert spaces and an identification is needed to relate the “perturbed” and “free” dynamics governed by
U_{t}
and
U^{0}_{t}
, respectively. First, we observe that given a pair of real states
X\in H_{c}
,
Y\in L_{c}^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{0})
such that
T_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathfrak{fr}[X])=a
and
\mathfrak{fr}[Y]=a
for some
a>0
, the Theorem
2.1
yields

\mathfrak{fr}[U_{t}X]=L_{\mathcal{H}}(|t|+a)=\mathfrak{fr}[(U_{t}^{0}Y)(T_{\mathcal{H}}(\cdot))],\qquad t\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\{0\}.

Thus, when
t
varies in the interval
(0,t_{0})
, the wavefronts of
U_{t}X
and
(U^{0}_{t}Y)(T_{\mathcal{H}}(\cdot))
simultaneously propagate from
L_{\mathcal{H}}(a)
to
L_{\mathcal{H}}(t_{0}+a)
. That provides an intuition how to map
U_{t}^{0}Y
into
L^{2}(\mathcal{H})
. First, we introduce the non-negative matrix-function
\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{n}}
:

\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\tau)=\begin{cases}\Bigl(\det\mathcal{H}(\tau)\Bigr)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{H}(\tau),&\tau:\det\mathcal{H}(\tau)>0,\\
\infty,&\tau:\det\mathcal{H}(\tau)=0.\end{cases}

Then,

(2.68)

\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{n}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\tau)=\begin{cases}{\Bigl(\det\mathcal{H}(\tau)\Bigr)}^{\frac{1}{4}}\mathcal{H}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\tau),&\tau:\det\mathcal{H}(\tau)>0,\\
0,&\tau:\det\mathcal{H}(\tau)=0.\end{cases}

It is instructive to note that
\det\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\tau)=1
for every
\tau
that satisfies
\det\mathcal{H}(\tau)>0
.
Second, we fix a measurable function
\gamma\colon\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{T}
. Finally, for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
Y\in L^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{0})
, define

(2.69)

\widetilde{U}_{\gamma,t}^{0}Y\colon\tau\mapsto\begin{cases}\gamma(\tau)\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{n}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\tau)(U_{t}^{0}Y)(T_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau)),\qquad t\geqslant 0,\\
\overline{\gamma(\tau)}\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{n}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\tau)(U_{t}^{0}Y)(T_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau)),\qquad t<0.\end{cases}

The role of the “phase function”
\gamma
will become clear in Theorem
2.19
below. Given definition (
2.69
), we get several important properties of the evolution
\widetilde{U}_{\gamma,t}^{0}
:

(A)

the dynamics
\widetilde{U}_{\gamma,t}^{0}Y
has an explicit expression in terms of
Y
,
\mathcal{H}
,
\gamma
;

Indeed, that follows from the explicit formula for
U_{t}^{0}Y
which we obtain in Lemma
2.21
below.

(B)

if
\mathfrak{fr}[X]=\mathfrak{fr}[Y(T_{\mathcal{H}}(\cdot))]
for some real states
X\in H_{c}
,
Y\in L_{c}^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{0})
, then

\mathfrak{fr}[\widetilde{U}_{\gamma,t}^{0}Y]=\mathfrak{fr}[U_{t}X],\qquad t\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\{0\}.

That is the direct consequence of Theorem
2.1
.

(C)

The map
\widetilde{U}_{\gamma,t}^{0}
is an isometry:

\|\widetilde{U}_{\gamma,t}^{0}Y\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}^{2}=\|Y\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{0})}^{2}.

The last relation comes from a change of variables:

\|\widetilde{U}_{\gamma,t}^{0}Y\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}T_{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}(\tau)\bigl\langle(U_{t}^{0}Y)(T_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau)),(U_{t}^{0}Y)(T_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau))\bigr\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}\,d\tau=\|U_{t}^{0}Y\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{0})}^{2}=\|Y\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{0})}^{2},

where
T_{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}=\sqrt{\det\mathcal{H}}
is the derivative of the locally absolutely continuous function
T_{\mathcal{H}}
.

(D)

The map
\widetilde{U}_{\gamma,t}^{0}
sends
L^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{0})
into
H
.

Indeed, for every
Y\in L^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{0})
we have
\widetilde{U}_{\gamma,t}^{0}Y=0
on each indivisible interval. Therefore,
\mathop{\mathrm{Ran}}\nolimits\widetilde{U}_{\gamma,t}^{0}\subseteq H
.

We aim to prove the following result.

Theorem 2.19
.

Let
\mathcal{H}\in\mathbf{Sz}{\rm(CS)}
. Then, there exists a function
\gamma\colon\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{T}
such that the following assertion holds. For every
X\in H
, there are unique
Y_{X,\pm}\in L^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{0})
such that for every
b>0
, we have

(2.70)

\lim_{t\to\pm\infty}\|U_{t}X-\widetilde{U}^{0}_{\gamma,t}Y_{X,\pm}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},\Delta_{b,t})}=0,

where
\Delta_{b,t}=\bigl[L_{\mathcal{H}}(|t|-b),L_{\mathcal{H}}(|t|+b)\bigr]
.
These
Y_{X,\pm}
can be computed by the formulas

(2.71)

Y_{X,+}=\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}^{-1}(f_{\bf ac}\overline{D_{\mu}}),\quad Y_{X,-}=\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}^{-1}(f_{\bf ac}{D_{\mu}}),

where
f=\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}}X,f_{\bf ac}:=f\cdot\chi_{\Omega_{\bf ac}(\mu)}
. Moreover, if
\mathcal{H}
is diagonal, then one can take
\gamma=1
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

Remark 2.20
.

In Theorem
2.19
, we do not assume that
X
has compact support or belongs to the absolutely continuous subspace of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{H}}
.

We start with providing an explicit formula for the evolution
U_{t}^{0}
. Let
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}
be the self-adjoint operator on
L^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{0})=L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})
corresponding to
\mathcal{H}_{0}
. We have
U_{t}^{0}=e^{it\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}}
. The main spectral measure of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}
is equal to the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}
and

(\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}Y)(z)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Bigl\langle\left(\begin{smallmatrix}y_{1}(\tau)\\
y_{2}(\tau)\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\cos(\tau\bar{z})\\
-\sin(\tau\bar{z})\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)\Bigr\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}d\tau,\qquad z\in\mathbb{C},

for every function
Y=\begin{pmatrix}y_{1}\\
y_{2}\end{pmatrix}
in
L_{c}^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})=L_{c}^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{0})
. Recall that
\widehat{h}
denotes the Fourier transform of a function
h\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

Lemma 2.21
.

Let
h\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
Y=\begin{pmatrix}y_{1}\\
y_{2}\end{pmatrix}\in L^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{0})
be defined by
\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}Y=h
. Extend
y_{1}
to all of
\mathbb{R}
as an even function and
y_{2}
as an odd function. Then, for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
\tau\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, we have

(2.72)

\displaystyle(U_{t}^{0}Y)(\tau)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\widehat{h}(\tau-t)\begin{pmatrix}1\\
-i\end{pmatrix}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\widehat{h}(-(\tau+t))\begin{pmatrix}1\\
i\end{pmatrix}

(2.73)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}y_{1}(\tau-t)+y_{1}(\tau+t)\\
-i(y_{1}(\tau-t)-y_{1}(\tau+t))\end{pmatrix}+\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}i(y_{2}(\tau-t)-y_{2}(\tau+t))\\
y_{2}(\tau-t)+y_{2}(\tau+t)\end{pmatrix},

where the integrals are understood in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
-sense. In particular, we have

(2.74)

(U_{t}^{0}Y)(\tau)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\widehat{h}(\tau-t)\cdot\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
-i\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)+o(1),\quad(U_{-t}^{0}Y)(\tau)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\widehat{h}(-\tau+t)\cdot\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
i\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)+o(1),\\

when
t\to+\infty
and
o(1)
is understood in
L^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{0})
-sense.

Proof.
We claim that

(2.75)

(U_{t}^{0}Y)(\tau)=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i(t-\tau)x}h(x)\,dx\cdot\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
-i\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)+\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i(t+\tau)x}h(x)\,dx\cdot\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
i\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right),

where the integrals are understood in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
-sense. To prove (
2.75
), we first assume that
h\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. Denote the right-hand side of (
2.75
) by
Z_{t}
. Notice that the integrals in the right-hand side of (
2.75
) converge absolutely. We only need to check that the images of
U^{0}_{t}Y
and
Z_{t}
under
\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}
coincide. Indeed,

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}(U_{t}^{0}Y)(s)=

\displaystyle e^{its}h(s)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\lim_{r\to\infty}\int_{-r}^{r}e^{i\tau s}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i(t-\tau)x}h(x)\,dx\,d\tau

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\lim_{r\to\infty}\left(\int_{0}^{r}\Bigl\langle\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
-i\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\!\begin{smallmatrix}\cos(\tau s)\\
-\sin(\tau s)\\
\end{smallmatrix}\!\right)\Bigr\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i(t-\tau)x}h(x)\,dx\,d\tau\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2\pi}\lim_{r\to\infty}\left(\int_{0}^{r}\Bigl\langle\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
i\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\!\begin{smallmatrix}\cos(\tau s)\\
-\sin(\tau s)\\
\end{smallmatrix}\!\right)\Bigr\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i(t+\tau)x}h(x)\,dx\,d\tau\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}Z_{t})(s),

for almost all
s\in\mathbb{R}
where the limits are understood in the
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
-sense. Since
L^{1}(\mathbb{R})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
is dense in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, we can extend (
2.75
) to all of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
by continuity. The formula (
2.72
) is immediate from (
2.75
) if we use the notation for Fourier transform. Finally, taking
t=0
in (
2.72
) gives
y_{1}(\tau)=(\widehat{h}(\tau)+\widehat{h}(-\tau))/\sqrt{2}
,
y_{2}(\tau)=-i(\widehat{h}(\tau)-\widehat{h}(-\tau))/\sqrt{2}
and the formula (
2.73
) follows. Since
\lim_{t\to+\infty}\|\widehat{h}(-(\tau+t))\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}=0
, we get the first limit in (
2.74
). The second one can be proved similarly.
∎

The free dynamics
U_{t}^{0}
is known to be reducible to the shift operator on the real line. We recall that construction in Section
5.2
.

Proof of Theorem
2.19
.

Existence of
Y_{X,\pm}
.

From Lemma
2.17
, we know that the set
\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{H}}\cap H_{c}
is dense in
H
. We start the proof by considering
X\in\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{H}}\cap H_{c}
. Recall that
b>0
is a number and
\chi_{\Delta_{b,t}}
denotes the characteristic function of the interval
\Delta_{b,t}.
Put
f=\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}}X
and let
f_{\bf ac}=f\cdot\chi_{\Omega_{\bf ac}(\mu)},f_{\bf s}=f\cdot\chi_{\Omega_{\bf s}(\mu)}
. By the spectral theorem, for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}}(\chi_{\Delta_{b,t}}U_{t}X)(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{\Delta_{b,t}}\langle\mathcal{H}(\tau)U_{t}X(\tau),\Theta(\tau,\bar{\lambda})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}\,d\tau

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}(U_{t}X,\chi_{\Delta_{b,t}}\Theta(\cdot,\bar{\lambda}))_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}

(2.76)

\displaystyle=(e^{itx}f,k_{\mu,\Delta_{b,t},\lambda})_{L^{2}(\mu)},

where

(2.77)

k_{\mu,\Delta_{b,t},\lambda}(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}}(\chi_{\Delta_{b,t}}\Theta(\cdot,\bar{\lambda}))=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\Delta_{b,t}}\langle\mathcal{H}(\tau)\Theta(\tau,\bar{\lambda}),\Theta(\tau,x)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}\,d\tau,\qquad x\in\mathbb{R}.

We are going to study the asymptotic behavior of (
2.76
) when
t\to\pm\infty
using representation (
2.77
). Let
G
be the matrix function from (
2.42
). Fix
\tau\in\Delta_{b,t}
and set

(2.78)

G_{1}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\
-i&i\end{smallmatrix}\right),\quad\Psi_{1}=\frac{1}{2}G^{-1}G_{1}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right),\quad\Psi_{2}=\frac{1}{2}G^{-1}G_{1}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
1\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right).

Recall that
\widetilde{P}_{\tau}
,
\widetilde{P}^{*}_{\tau}
,
\alpha_{\tau}
are defined in (
2.44
). On the real line
\mathbb{R}
, we have

\displaystyle\Theta

\displaystyle=G^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\tilde{E}_{\tau}+\tilde{E}^{\sharp}_{\tau}}{2}\\
\frac{\tilde{E}_{\tau}-\tilde{E}^{\sharp}_{\tau}}{2i}\end{pmatrix}=\frac{1}{2}G^{-1}G_{1}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{E}_{\tau}\\
\tilde{E}^{\sharp}_{\tau}\end{pmatrix}=\frac{e^{-iT_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau)x}}{2}G^{-1}G_{1}\begin{pmatrix}\overline{\alpha}_{\tau}\widetilde{P}_{2\tau}^{*}\\
\alpha_{\tau}\widetilde{P}_{2\tau}\end{pmatrix},

(2.79)

\displaystyle=\overline{\alpha}_{\tau}e^{-iT_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau)x}\widetilde{P}_{2\tau}^{*}\Psi_{1}+{\alpha_{\tau}}e^{iT_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau)x}\overline{\widetilde{P}_{2\tau}^{*}}\Psi_{2}.

We continue by getting the estimates on
\|\Psi_{1}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},\Delta_{b,t})}
and
\|\Psi_{2}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},\Delta_{b,t})}
.
Note that

\displaystyle 4\langle\mathcal{H}\Psi_{1},\Psi_{1}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}+4\langle\mathcal{H}\Psi_{2},\Psi_{2}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\langle\mathcal{H}G^{-1}G_{1}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right),G^{-1}G_{1}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}+\langle\mathcal{H}G^{-1}G_{1}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
1\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right),G^{-1}G_{1}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
1\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{trace}}\nolimits G_{1}^{*}(G^{-1})^{*}\mathcal{H}G^{-1}G_{1}=2\mathop{\mathrm{trace}}\nolimits(G^{-1})^{*}\mathcal{H}G^{-1}

due to the fact that
G_{1}G_{1}^{*}=2\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
Using this calculation and relation (
2.43
), we get

\displaystyle 2\|\Psi_{1}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},\Delta_{b,t})}^{2}+2\|\Psi_{2}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},\Delta_{b,t})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Delta_{b,t}}\mathop{\mathrm{trace}}\nolimits{(G^{-1})^{*}\mathcal{H}G^{-1}}\,d\tau

\displaystyle=\|\mathcal{K}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{H}}\|_{L^{1}(\Delta_{b,t})}+2\int_{\Delta_{b,t}}\sqrt{\det\mathcal{H}}\,d\tau,

because
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{H}}
is a non-increasing function.
Note that if
|t|\geqslant b
, we have
\int_{\Delta_{b,t}}\sqrt{\det\mathcal{H}}\,d\tau=T_{\mathcal{H}}(L_{\mathcal{H}}(|t|+b))-T_{\mathcal{H}}(L_{\mathcal{H}}(|t|-b))=(|t|+b)-(|t|-b)=2b
.
Thus, for such
t
we have

(2.80)

2\|\Psi_{1}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},\Delta_{b,t})}^{2}+2\|\Psi_{2}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},\Delta_{b,t})}^{2}\leqslant\|\mathcal{K}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{H}}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}+2b.

Next, we study the inner product (
2.76
) using (
2.77
). We need some auxiliary bounds first. By applying the spectral theorem, we have
(x+i)f\in L^{2}(\mu)
. Relations (
2.54
) and (
2.55
), along with
Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, imply that

\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}|f_{\bf s}(x)\widetilde{P}_{2\tau}^{*}(x)|d\mu_{\mathbf{s}}\right)^{2}\leqslant\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}|f(x)|^{2}(x^{2}+1)\,d\mu\right)\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{|\widetilde{P}_{2\tau}^{*}(x)|^{2}}{x^{2}+1}\,d\mu_{\mathbf{s}}\right)\to 0,

and that

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}|f_{\bf ac}(x)(\widetilde{P}_{2\tau}^{*}(x)w(x)-\ \overline{D_{\mu}}(x))|\,dx\right)^{2}\leqslant

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}|f(x)|^{2}(x^{2}+1)d\mu\right)\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{|\widetilde{P}_{2\tau}^{*}(x)-D_{\mu}^{-1}(x)|^{2}}{x^{2}+1}w(x)\,dx\right)\to 0,

when
\tau\to\infty
. Since
\mathcal{H}\in\mathbf{Sz}{\rm(CS)}
, we get
\lim_{s\to\infty}L_{\mathcal{H}}(s)=+\infty
. Thus, recalling that
\mu=|D_{\mu}|^{2}\,dx+\mu_{\mathbf{s}}
, one has

(2.81)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i(t-T_{\mathcal{H}})x}f(x)\widetilde{P}_{2\tau}^{*}(x)\,d\mu(x)-\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i(t-T_{\mathcal{H}})x}f_{\bf ac}(x)\overline{D_{\mu}(x)}\,dx\right)=0

uniformly in
\tau\in\Delta_{b,t}
. Similarly,
(x+i)f\in L^{2}(\mu)
, relations (
2.54
), (
2.55
) and the Riemann-Lebesgue lemma imply

(2.82)

\lim_{t\to+\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i(t+T_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau))x}f(x)\overline{\widetilde{P}_{2\tau}^{*}(x)}\,d\mu(x)=\lim_{t\to+\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i(t+T_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau))x}f_{\bf ac}(x)\overline{D_{\mu}(x)}\,dx=0,

uniformly with respect to
\tau\in\Delta_{b,t}
. That follows from the inclusion
f_{\bf ac}\overline{D_{\mu}}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
which is immediate from our assumptions on
f
and the bound

\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}|f_{\bf ac}(x)D_{\mu}(x)|\,dx\right)^{2}\leqslant\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{dx}{x^{2}+1}\right)\cdot\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}|f(x)|(x^{2}+1)\,d\mu(x)\right)<\infty.

Taking into account (
2.76
) and (
2.79
), we see that
\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}}(\chi_{\Delta_{b,t}}U_{t}X)(\lambda)
equals

\displaystyle(e^{itx}f,k_{\mu,\Delta_{b,t},\lambda})_{L^{2}(\mu)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{itx}f(x)\overline{\int_{\Delta_{b,t}}\langle\mathcal{H}(\tau)\Theta(\tau,\bar{\lambda}),\Theta(\tau,x)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}\,d\tau}\,d\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\Delta_{b,t}}e^{itx}f(x)\langle\mathcal{H}(\tau)\Theta(\tau,x),\Theta(\tau,\bar{\lambda})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}\,d\tau\,d\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\Delta_{b,t}}e^{itx}f(x)\langle\mathcal{H}(\tau)[\overline{\alpha}_{\tau}e^{-iT_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau)x}\widetilde{P}_{2\tau}^{*}\Psi_{1}],\Theta(\tau,\bar{\lambda})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}\,d\tau\,d\mu(x)

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\Delta_{b,t}}e^{itx}f(x)\langle\mathcal{H}(\tau)[\alpha_{\tau}e^{iT_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau)x}\overline{\widetilde{P}_{2\tau}^{*}}\Psi_{2}],\Theta(\tau,\bar{\lambda})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}\,d\tau\,d\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\Delta_{b,t}}\left(\overline{\alpha}_{\tau}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i(t-T_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau))x}f_{\bf ac}(x)\overline{D_{\mu}}(x)\,dx\right)\left\langle\mathcal{H}(\tau)\Psi_{1},\Theta(\tau,\bar{\lambda})\right\rangle d\tau+R(t,\lambda),

where
R(t,\lambda)=R_{1}(t,\lambda)+R_{2}(t,\lambda)
,

R_{1}(t,\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\Delta_{b,t}}\overline{\alpha}_{\tau}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i(t-T_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau))x}f\widetilde{P}_{2\tau}^{*}\,d\mu(x)-\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i(t-T_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau))x}f_{\bf ac}\overline{D_{\mu}}\,dx\right)\left\langle\mathcal{H}(\tau)\Psi_{1},\Theta(\tau,\bar{\lambda})\right\rangle d\tau,

and

R_{2}(t,\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\Delta_{b,t}}\alpha_{\tau}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i(t+T_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau))x}f(x)\overline{\widetilde{P}_{2\tau}^{*}}(x)\,d\mu(x)\right)\left\langle\mathcal{H}(\tau)\Psi_{2},\Theta(\tau,\bar{\lambda})\right\rangle d\tau.

Observe that by (
2.81
) and (
2.82
) both
R_{1}(t,\lambda)
and
R_{2}(t,\lambda)
can be represented in the form

R_{1,2}(t,\lambda)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{\Delta_{b,t}}\left\langle\mathcal{H}(\tau)\psi_{1,2}(t,\tau),\Theta(\tau,\bar{\lambda})\right\rangle d\tau=\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{H}}(\Delta_{b,t}\psi_{1,2})(\lambda),

where
\psi_{1,2}(t,\cdot)=\kappa_{1,2}(t,\cdot)\Psi_{1,2}
for some functions
\kappa_{1,2}(t,\cdot)
such that
\lim_{t\to+\infty}\|\kappa_{1,2}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}(\Delta_{b,t})}=0
. Estimate (
2.80
) shows that the quantities
\|\Psi_{1,2}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},\Delta_{b,t})}
are uniformly bounded with respect to
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, hence
\lim_{t\to+\infty}\|\psi_{1,2}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},\Delta_{b,t})}=0
.
Therefore, we have
\lim_{t\to+\infty}\|R(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0
by Lemma
2.18
. Summarizing, we see that

\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}}(\chi_{\Delta_{b,t}}U_{t}X)=\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\frac{\overline{\alpha}_{\tau}\chi_{\Delta_{b,t}}}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i(t-T_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau))x}f_{\bf ac}(x)\overline{D_{\mu}(x)}\,dx\cdot G^{-1}(\tau)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
-i\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)+o(1),

as
t\to+\infty
with
o(1)
in
L^{2}(\mu)
(the function under
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{H}}
depends on
\tau
).
Applying
\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}
and using Lemma
2.18
, we get

(2.83)

(\chi_{\Delta_{b,t}}U_{t}X)(\tau)=P_{H}\left(\frac{\overline{\alpha}_{\tau}\chi_{\Delta_{b,t}}}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i(t-T_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau))x}f_{\bf ac}(x)\overline{D_{\mu}(x)}\,dx\cdot G^{-1}(\tau)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
-i\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)+o(1),

with
o(1)
in
H
. Similar reasoning gives

(\chi_{\Delta_{b,t}}U_{t}X)(\tau)=P_{H}\left(\frac{\alpha_{\tau}\chi_{\Delta_{b,t}}}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i(t+T_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau))x}f_{\bf ac}(x)D_{\mu}(x)\,dx\cdot G^{-1}(\tau)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
i\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)+o(1),

when
t\to-\infty
.

Having established this asymptotics, we want to relate the integral in the right-hand side of (
2.83
) to the free evolution one finds in (
2.70
). To this end, we first define
Y_{X,+}\in L^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{0})
by the relations
\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}Y_{X,+}=f_{\bf ac}\overline{D_{\mu}}
. By the spectral theorem, we have
(x+i)f\in L^{2}(\mu)
. So, the inclusion
f_{\bf ac}\overline{D_{\mu}}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R})

and property
(D)
shows that
\chi_{\Delta_{b,t}}\widetilde{U}^{0}_{\gamma,t}Y_{X,+}
can be considered as an element of
H
for every choice of the phase function
\gamma
.
To understand this function better, we notice that (
2.75
) implies

(2.84)

(U_{t}^{0}Y_{X,+})(T_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau))=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i(t-T_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau))x}f_{\bf ac}\overline{D_{\mu}}\,dx\cdot\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
-i\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)+\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i(t+T_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau))x}f_{\bf ac}\overline{D_{\mu}}\,dx\cdot\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
i\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right),

where the integrals converge absolutely. Riemann-Lebesgue lemma gives

(2.85)

\lim_{t\to+\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i(t+T_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau))x}f_{\bf ac}(x)\overline{D_{\mu}(x)}\,dx=0

uniformly with respect to
\tau\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Next, we indicate how the phase function
\gamma
is chosen in (
2.70
).
For a.e.
\tau\geqslant 0
, we have
\mathcal{H}(\tau)\geqslant 0
and
\det G(\tau)=1
so Lemma
5.2
allows us to choose
\varphi(\tau)\in[0,2\pi)
such that

(2.86)

\sqrt{\mathcal{H}(\tau)}G^{-1}(\tau)\Sigma_{\varphi(\tau)}^{-1}\geqslant 0.

That
\varphi
is Lebesgue-measurable.
Observe that for
u\in[0,2\pi)
we have

e^{iu}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
-i\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)=(\cos u+i\sin u)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
-i\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\Sigma_{-u}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
-i\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\Sigma_{u}^{-1}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
-i\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right).

We can then choose measurable
\gamma
so that
|\gamma(\tau)|=1
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
and

(2.87)

\gamma(\tau)\Sigma_{\varphi(\tau)}^{-1}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
-i\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\overline{\alpha}_{\tau}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
-i\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right),

i.e.,

(2.88)

\gamma(\tau)=\overline{\alpha}_{\tau}e^{-i\varphi(\tau)}.

Note also that for the rotation matrix
\Sigma_{u}
from Lemma
5.2
we can use the definition of
\Psi_{1}
and
\Psi_{2}
in (
2.78
) to get

(2.89)

\sup_{u\in[0,2\pi]}\|\chi_{\Delta_{b,t}}G^{-1}\Sigma_{u}^{-1}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
i\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}\leqslant 4\|\Psi_{1}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},\Delta_{b,t})}+4\|\Psi_{2}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},\Delta_{b,t})},

where the right-hand side is uniformly bounded by (
2.80
). Then, (
2.84
), (
2.85
), and (
2.89
) imply

\displaystyle\gamma(\tau)\chi_{\Delta_{b,t}}G^{-1}\Sigma_{\varphi(\tau)}^{-1}(U_{t}^{0}Y_{X,+})(T_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau))

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma(\tau)\chi_{\Delta_{b,t}}}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i(t-T_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau))x}f_{\bf ac}\overline{D_{\mu}}\,dx\cdot G^{-1}(\tau)\Sigma_{\varphi(\tau)}^{-1}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
-i\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)+o(1),

(2.90)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{sa8}}}{{=}}\frac{\overline{\alpha}_{\tau}\chi_{\Delta_{b,t}}}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i(t-T_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau))x}f_{\bf ac}\overline{D_{\mu}}\,dx\cdot G^{-1}(\tau)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
-i\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)+o(1),

with
o(1)
in
L^{2}(\mathcal{H})
as
t\to+\infty
. Combining this with (
2.83
), we obtain

(2.91)

\chi_{\Delta_{b,t}}(\tau)U_{t}X(\tau)-P_{H}\left(\gamma(\tau)\chi_{\Delta_{b,t}}(\tau)G^{-1}\Sigma_{\varphi(\tau)}^{-1}(U_{t}^{0}Y_{X,+})(T_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau))\right)\to 0,\qquad t\to+\infty,

in
H
. Similarly, for the same choice of
\gamma
and
Y_{X,-}
defined by
\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}Y_{X,-}=f_{\bf ac}D_{\mu}
we have

\overline{\gamma(\tau)}\Sigma_{\varphi(\tau)}^{-1}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
i\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\alpha_{\tau}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
i\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right),

by taking conjugation of (
2.87
) and

\chi_{\Delta_{b,t}}(\tau)U_{t}X(\tau)-P_{H}\left(\overline{\gamma(\tau)}\chi_{\Delta_{b,t}}(\tau)G^{-1}\Sigma_{\varphi(\tau)}^{-1}(U_{t}^{0}Y_{X,-})(T_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau))\right)\to 0,\qquad t\to-\infty,

in
H
. Consider the set
\Delta_{b,t}^{0}=\Delta_{b,t}\cap\{\tau\colon\det\mathcal{H}(\tau)=0\}
and denote
\Delta_{b,t}^{\prime}=\Delta_{b,t}\setminus\Delta_{b,t}^{0}
. Recall the formula (
2.90
) and note that

\|\chi_{\Delta_{b,t}^{0}}G^{-1}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
-i\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}^{2}=4\|\Psi_{1}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},\Delta_{b,t}^{0})}^{2}.

Similarly to (
2.80
), we have

2\|\Psi_{1}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},\Delta_{b,t}^{0})}^{2}\leqslant\|\mathcal{K}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{H}}\|_{L^{1}(\Delta_{b,t}^{0})}+2\int_{\Delta_{b,t}^{0}}\sqrt{\det\mathcal{H}}\,d\tau=\|\mathcal{K}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{H}}\|_{L^{1}(\Delta_{b,t}^{0})},

which tends to zero as
t\to+\infty
thanks to (
2.41
). Together with (
2.83
) this yields

(2.92)

\lim_{t\to+\infty}\|\chi_{\Delta_{b,t}^{0}}\cdot U_{t}X\|_{H}=0.

We also have
\|\chi_{\Delta_{b,t}^{0}}\cdot\widetilde{U}^{0}_{t,\gamma}Y_{X,+}\|_{H}=0

by the definition of
\widetilde{U}^{0}_{t,\gamma}
. From (
2.91
), it is now clear that the relation

(2.93)

\|U_{t}X-\widetilde{U}^{0}_{t,\gamma}Y_{X,+}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},\Delta_{b,t})}\to 0,\qquad t\to+\infty,

is equivalent to the relation

\|P_{H}\left(\gamma\chi_{\Delta_{b,t}}G^{-1}\Sigma_{\varphi}^{-1}(U_{t}^{0}Y_{X,+})(T_{\mathcal{H}}(\cdot))\right)-\widetilde{U}^{0}_{t,\gamma}Y_{X,+}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},\Delta_{b,t}^{\prime})}\to 0,\qquad t\to+\infty.

Since
\widetilde{U}^{0}_{t,\gamma}Y_{X,+}
belongs to
H
by the definition of
\widetilde{U}^{0}_{t,\gamma}
, we have
P_{H}\widetilde{U}^{0}_{t,\gamma}Y_{X,+}=\widetilde{U}^{0}_{t,\gamma}Y_{X,+}
. Moreover, one gets
\|P_{H}Y\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},\Delta_{b,t}^{\prime})}=\|Y\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},\Delta_{b,t}^{\prime})}
for every
Y\in L^{2}(\mathcal{H})
, because the operator
Y\mapsto\chi_{\Delta_{b,t}^{\prime}}Y
is the orthogonal projector in
L^{2}(\mathcal{H})
onto a subspace in
H
. Therefore, (
2.93
) will follow if we prove

\|\gamma G^{-1}\Sigma_{\varphi}^{-1}(U_{t}^{0}Y_{X,+})(T_{\mathcal{H}}(\cdot))-\gamma\sqrt[4]{\det\mathcal{H}}\mathcal{H}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(U_{t}^{0}Y_{X,+})(T_{\mathcal{H}}(\cdot))\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},\Delta_{b,t}^{\prime})}\to 0,

as
t\to+\infty
. Similarly,

\|U_{t}X-\widetilde{U}^{0}_{t,\gamma}Y_{X,-}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},\Delta_{b,t})}\to 0,\qquad t\to-\infty,

follows from

\|\bar{\gamma}G^{-1}\Sigma_{\varphi}^{-1}(U_{t}^{0}Y_{X,-})(T_{\mathcal{H}}(\cdot))-\bar{\gamma}\sqrt[4]{\det\mathcal{H}}\mathcal{H}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(U_{t}^{0}Y_{X,-})(T_{\mathcal{H}}(\cdot))\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},\Delta_{b,t}^{\prime})}\to 0,

as
t\to-\infty
. Since
|\gamma|=1
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, we only need to prove

(2.94)

\|G^{-1}\Sigma_{\varphi}^{-1}(U_{t}^{0}Y_{X,\pm})(T_{\mathcal{H}}(\cdot))-\sqrt[4]{\det\mathcal{H}}\mathcal{H}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(U_{t}^{0}Y_{X,\pm})(T_{\mathcal{H}}(\cdot))\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},\Delta_{b,t}^{\prime})}\to 0,

where
t\to\pm\infty
. Noting that
\|X\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},\Delta_{b,t}^{\prime})}=\|\sqrt{\mathcal{H}}X\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{0},\Delta_{b,t}^{\prime})}
, we see that the norm in (
2.94
) is equal to

\displaystyle\ldots

\displaystyle=\|(G^{-1}\Sigma_{\varphi}^{-1}-\sqrt[4]{\det\mathcal{H}}\mathcal{H}^{-\frac{1}{2}})(U_{t}^{0}Y_{X,\pm})(T_{\mathcal{H}}(\cdot))\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},\Delta_{b,t}^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=\bigl\|\bigl[\sqrt{\mathcal{H}}G^{-1}\Sigma_{\varphi}^{-1}-\sqrt[4]{\det\mathcal{H}}\bigr](U_{t}^{0}Y_{X,\pm})(T_{\mathcal{H}}(\cdot))\bigr\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{0},\Delta_{b,t}^{\prime})}.

Thus, (
2.94
) can be rewritten further in the form

(2.95)

\bigl\|\bigl[V-\sqrt{\det V}\bigr](U^{0}_{t}Y_{X,\pm})(T_{\mathcal{H}}(\cdot))\bigr\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{0},\Delta_{b,t}^{\prime})}\to 0,

where the matrix-function
V
is defined by
V=\sqrt{\mathcal{H}}G^{-1}\Sigma_{\varphi}^{-1}
. Recall that
V\geqslant 0
by the choice of
\varphi
we made in (
2.86
).
For each
\tau\in\Delta_{b,t}^{\prime}
, let
e_{1}(\tau)
and
e_{2}(\tau)
denote the orthonormal eigenvectors of
V(\tau)
corresponding to the eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}(\tau)
and
\lambda_{2}(\tau)
. Then, for every vector
e=c_{1}e_{1}(\tau)+c_{2}e_{2}(\tau)
in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\|(V-\sqrt{\det V})e\|_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=(\lambda_{1}(\tau)-\sqrt{\lambda_{1}(\tau)\lambda_{2}(\tau)})^{2}|c_{1}|^{2}+(\lambda_{2}(\tau)-\sqrt{\lambda_{1}(\tau)\lambda_{2}(\tau)})^{2}|c_{2}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant(\lambda_{1}(\tau)-\lambda_{2}(\tau))^{2}\|e\|_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}^{2},

due to the fact that

(a-\sqrt{ab})^{2}+(b-\sqrt{ab})^{2}=(a+b)(\sqrt{a}-\sqrt{b})^{2}\leqslant(\sqrt{a}+\sqrt{b})^{2}(\sqrt{a}-\sqrt{b})^{2}=(a-b)^{2}.

On the other hand,

(\lambda_{1}(\tau)-\lambda_{2}(\tau))^{2}=\mathop{\mathrm{trace}}\nolimits(V^{2})-2\det V.

Since
V^{2}=V^{*}V
, we can write

\mathop{\mathrm{trace}}\nolimits V^{2}=\mathop{\mathrm{trace}}\nolimits(\Sigma_{\varphi}(G^{-1})^{*}{\mathcal{H}}G^{-1}\Sigma_{\varphi}^{-1})=\mathop{\mathrm{trace}}\nolimits((G^{-1})^{*}\mathcal{H}G^{-1})
.
So,

(\lambda_{1}(\tau)-\lambda_{2}(\tau))^{2}=\mathop{\mathrm{trace}}\nolimits((G^{-1})^{*}\mathcal{H}G^{-1})-2\sqrt{\det\mathcal{H}}=-\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime},

as follows from (
2.43
). Since
U^{0}_{t}Y_{X,\pm}\in L^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{0})
,
\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}\nolimits(0,\Delta_{b,t}^{\prime})\to\infty
, and
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{H}}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
, we see that (
2.95
) holds. Hence,
Y_{X,\pm}
satisfy (
2.70
).

Now, consider the case where
X\in H
is an arbitrary element (that is, we do not assume now that
X\in\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{H}}\cap H_{c}
). Lemma
2.17
allows us to find
X_{n}\in\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{H}}\cap H_{c}
such that

X=\lim_{n\to\infty}X_{n}

and this limit is in
L^{2}(\mathcal{H})
-norm.
Let
Y_{X_{n},\pm}
be the corresponding elements of
L^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{0})
: if
f_{n}=\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}}(X_{n})
, then
Y_{X_{n},+}:=\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}^{-1}(f_{n}\overline{D_{\mu}}\cdot\chi_{\Omega_{\bf ac}(\mu)})
,
Y_{X_{n},-}:=\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}^{-1}(f_{n}{D_{\mu}}\cdot\chi_{\Omega_{\bf ac}(\mu)})
, and

\|Y_{X_{n},+}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{0})}=\|\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}(Y_{X_{n},+})\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=\|f_{n}\overline{D_{\mu}}\cdot\chi_{\Omega_{\bf ac}(\mu)}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant\|f_{n}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=\|X_{n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}.

A similar relation holds for
Y_{X_{n},-}
. Since
\{X_{n}\}
converges to
X
, the sequence
f_{n}
converges to
f=\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}}X
in
L^{2}(\mu)
.
In particular,
(f_{n}-f)\overline{D}_{\mu}\cdot\chi_{\Omega_{\bf ac}(\mu)}\to 0
and
(f_{n}-f)D_{\mu}\cdot\chi_{\Omega_{\bf ac}(\mu)}\to 0
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. The sequences
\{Y_{X_{n},\pm}\}
converge and we denote
Y_{X,\pm}=\lim_{n\to\infty}Y_{X_{n},\pm}
. In fact,
Y_{X,+}=\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}^{-1}(f\overline{D_{\mu}}\cdot\chi_{\Omega_{\bf ac}(\mu)})
and
Y_{X,-}=\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}^{-1}(f{D_{\mu}}\cdot\chi_{\Omega_{\bf ac}(\mu)})
, which proves (
2.71
).
Moreover, for each
t
we have

\|\widetilde{U}^{0}_{\gamma,t}(Y_{X_{n},\pm})-\widetilde{U}^{0}_{\gamma,t}(Y_{X,\pm})\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}^{2}=\|Y_{X_{n},\pm}-Y_{X,\pm}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{0})}^{2},

by
(C)
. Now, given that (
2.70
) holds for every
X_{n}
, we can extend (
2.70
) to all
X
by the standard approximation argument.

Uniqueness of
Y_{X,\pm}
.

We will prove uniqueness of
Y_{X,+}
, the argument for
Y_{X,-}
is similar. Suppose that
Y_{X,+}
and
\widetilde{Y}_{X,+}
both satisfy (
2.70
) for some
X\in H
. Denote
Y_{0}=Y_{X,+}-\widetilde{Y}_{X,+}
and let
h
be such that
\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}Y_{0}=h
. We have

\lim_{t\to+\infty}\|\widetilde{U}_{\gamma,t}^{0}Y_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},\Delta_{b,t})}=0,\qquad\Delta_{b,t}=\bigl[L_{\mathcal{H}}(|t|-b),L_{\mathcal{H}}(|t|+b)\bigr],

for every
b>0
. By Lemma
2.21
, we have

(2.96)

U_{t}^{0}Y_{0}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\widehat{h}(\tau-t)\cdot\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
-i\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\widehat{h}(-(\tau+t))\cdot\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
i\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right).

Then,

\lim_{t\to+\infty}\int_{L_{\mathcal{H}}(t-b)}^{L_{\mathcal{H}}(t+b)}\sqrt{\det\mathcal{H}(\tau)}(|\widehat{h}(T_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau)-t)+\widehat{h}(-(T_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau)+t))|^{2}+|\widehat{h}(T_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau)-t)-\widehat{h}(-(T_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau)+t))|^{2})\,d\tau=0,

and, after changing variables,

0=\lim_{t\to+\infty}\int_{t-b}^{t+b}|\widehat{h}(s-t)|^{2}\,ds=\int_{-b}^{b}|\widehat{h}(\alpha)|^{2}\,d\alpha.

Since
b
is arbitrary, we get
\widehat{h}=0
a.e. on
\mathbb{R}
. Hence,
h=0
a.e. which gives
Y_{0}=0
and so
Y_{X,+}
is defined uniquely by
X
.

To complete the proof, it remains to check that
\alpha_{\tau}=1
and
G(\tau)>0
for every
\tau\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
in the case when
\mathcal{H}
is diagonal. Then,
\gamma(\tau)=1
as well by (
2.88
).
We have
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau)=0
,
\tau\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, for any diagonal Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}
, see Lemma 2.2 in
[
6
]
. Then,
G(\tau)
is a diagonal matrix with positive entries for every
\tau\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, in particular,
G(\tau)>0
. Suppose for a moment that
\det\mathcal{H}=1
almost everywhere on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
Then, formula
(42)
in
[
9
]
for
z=i
together with the relation
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{H}}=0
says that
(e^{-\tau}\tilde{E}_{\tau}(i))^{\prime}=-\mathcal{K}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau)e^{-\tau}\tilde{E}_{\tau}(i)
. Since
\tilde{E}_{0}(i)>0
, this shows that for such Hamiltonians
\mathcal{H}
we have
\tilde{E}_{\tau}(i)>0
,
\tau\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
That implies
\alpha_{\tau}=1
for all
\tau\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Now let
\mathcal{H}\in\mathbf{Sz}{\rm(CS)}
be a diagonal Hamiltonian such that
\det\mathcal{H}>0
almost everywhere on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Define the new Hamiltonian
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}\colon\tau\mapsto(\det\mathcal{H}(L_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau)))^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{H}(L_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau))
,
\det\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}=1
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
The function
\tau\mapsto\Theta(L_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau),z)
then solves Cauchy problem (
2.5
) for
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}
. The previous reasoning shows that for the corresponding coefficient
\widetilde{\alpha}_{\tau}
we have
\widetilde{\alpha}_{\tau}=1
,
\tau\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. But
\widetilde{\alpha}_{\tau}=\alpha_{L_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau)}
, and we see that
\alpha_{\tau}=1
for all diagonal Hamiltonians
\mathcal{H}\in\mathbf{Sz}{\rm(CS)}
such that
\det\mathcal{H}>0
almost everywhere on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
Then, the general case follows via an approximation argument by considering Hamiltonians of the form
\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)+\mathcal{H}
, and letting
\varepsilon\to 0
. ∎

2.6.
Scattering and wave operators

The following theorem answers the question: does the asymptotics
Y_{X,\pm}
of a state
X
under the evolution
U_{t}
determine the state
X
itself? It also strengthens Theorem
2.19
.

Theorem 2.22
.

Let
\mathcal{H}\in\mathbf{Sz}{\rm(CS)}
, let
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{H}}
be the corresponding self-adjoint operator (
2.4
) on
H
, and let
\mu=w\,dx+\mu_{\mathbf{s}}
be the main spectral measure for
\mathcal{H}
. Then, the strong wave operators

(2.97)

W_{\pm}=\lim_{t\to\pm\infty}U_{t}^{-1}\widetilde{U}^{0}_{\gamma,t}

exist and are complete, i.e., they are correctly defined (the limits are understood in the strong operator topology) and unitary as operators from
L^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{0})
onto the absolutely continuous subspace
H_{\bf ac}
of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{H}}
. Moreover, if
X\in H
,
Y_{X,\pm}
are defined by (
2.71
) and
P_{\bf ac}
denotes the orthogonal projection in
H
onto the absolutely continuous subspace of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{H}}
, then we have
W_{\pm}Y_{X,\pm}=P_{\bf ac}X
. Hence,
Y_{X,\pm}
determine
P_{\bf ac}X
uniquely and we have
Y_{X_{1},\pm}=Y_{X_{2},\pm}
in Theorem
2.19
if and only if
P_{\bf ac}X_{1}=P_{\bf ac}X_{2}
.
The scattering operator

S=W_{+}^{-1}W_{-},\qquad S\colon Y_{X,-}\mapsto Y_{X,+},

is a unitary operator on
L^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{0})
, and its spectral representation takes the form

(2.98)

\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}S\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}^{-1}f_{0}=\frac{\overline{D}_{\mu}}{D_{\mu}}f_{0},\qquad f_{0}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),

where
D_{\mu}
is the Szegő function of
\mu
. In particular, the operator
S
does not depend on the choice of the phase function
\gamma
in Theorem
2.19
.

Proof.
Let us first prove that the limit in (
2.97
) exists as
t\to+\infty
. The argument for
t\to-\infty
is similar. In fact, we claim that for an arbitrary
X
, we have

(2.99)

\lim_{t\to+\infty}U_{t}^{-1}\widetilde{U}^{0}_{\gamma,t}Y_{X,+}=P_{\bf ac}X.

Indeed, denoting
\Delta_{b,t}=[L_{\mathcal{H}}(|t|-b),L_{\mathcal{H}}(|t|+b)]
for some positive numbers
b
, we have

\displaystyle U_{t}^{-1}\widetilde{U}^{0}_{\gamma,t}Y_{X,+}

\displaystyle=U_{t}^{-1}\chi_{\Delta_{b,t}}\widetilde{U}^{0}_{\gamma,t}Y_{X,+}+U_{t}^{-1}\chi_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\setminus\Delta_{b,t}}\widetilde{U}^{0}_{\gamma,t}Y_{X,+}

\displaystyle=U_{t}^{-1}\chi_{\Delta_{b,t}}U_{t}X+U_{t}^{-1}(\chi_{\Delta_{b,t}}\widetilde{U}^{0}_{\gamma,t}Y_{X,+}-\chi_{\Delta_{b,t}}U_{t}X)+U_{t}^{-1}\chi_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\setminus\Delta_{b,t}}\widetilde{U}^{0}_{\gamma,t}Y_{X,+}.

To get our claim, it is enough to prove that

(a)

\lim_{b\to+\infty}\limsup_{t\to+\infty}\|\chi_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\setminus\Delta_{b,t}}\widetilde{U}^{0}_{\gamma,t}Y_{X,+}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}=0,

(b)

\lim_{t\to+\infty}\|\chi_{\Delta_{b,t}}\widetilde{U}^{0}_{\gamma,t}Y_{X,+}-\chi_{\Delta_{b,t}}U_{t}X\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}=0
for every
b\geqslant 0
,

(c)

\lim_{b\to+\infty}\limsup_{t\to+\infty}\|U_{t}^{-1}\chi_{\Delta_{b,t}}U_{t}X-P_{\bf ac}X\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}=0
.

Clearly,
(b)
is just a restatement of Theorem
2.19
. To check
(a)
, observe that

\displaystyle\|\chi_{\mathbb{R}\setminus\Delta_{b,t}}\widetilde{U}^{0}_{\gamma,t}Y_{X,+}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\setminus\Delta_{b,t}}\sqrt{\det\mathcal{H}(\tau)}\|U^{0}_{t}Y_{X,+}(T_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau))\|_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}^{2}\,d\tau

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\setminus[|t|-b,|t|+b]}\|U^{0}_{t}Y_{X,+}(\tau)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}^{2}\,d\tau.

Now, (
2.74
) yields
(a)
. It remains to prove
(c)
. First, notice that

\|U_{t}^{-1}\chi_{\Delta_{b,t}}U_{t}X-P_{\bf ac}X\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}=\|\chi_{\Delta_{b,t}}(U_{t}X-U_{t}P_{\bf ac}X)\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}^{2}+\|\chi_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\backslash\Delta_{b,t}}U_{t}P_{\bf ac}X\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}^{2}.

By Theorem
2.19
, we have
Y_{X,+}=Y_{P_{\bf ac}X,+}
and, therefore,

\lim_{t\to+\infty}\|\chi_{\Delta_{b,t}}(U_{t}X-U_{t}P_{\bf ac}X)\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}=0.

Finally,

\displaystyle\|\chi_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\backslash\Delta_{b,t}}U_{t}P_{\bf ac}X\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}

\displaystyle=\|U_{t}P_{\bf ac}X\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}-\|\chi_{\Delta_{b,t}}U_{t}P_{\bf ac}X\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}

\displaystyle=\|P_{\bf ac}X\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}-\|\chi_{\Delta_{b,t}}U_{t}P_{\bf ac}X\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})},

and, by Theorem
2.19
,

\displaystyle\limsup_{t\to+\infty}(\|P_{\bf ac}X\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}-\|\chi_{\Delta_{b,t}}U_{t}P_{\bf ac}X\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})})=\|P_{\bf ac}X\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}-\liminf_{t\to+\infty}\|\chi_{\Delta_{b,t}}\widetilde{U}^{0}_{\gamma,t}Y_{P_{\bf ac}X,+}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}

\displaystyle=\|P_{\bf ac}X\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}-\liminf_{t\to+\infty}(\|\widetilde{U}^{0}_{\gamma,t}Y_{P_{\bf ac}X,+}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}-\|\chi_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\backslash\Delta_{b,t}}\widetilde{U}^{0}_{\gamma,t}Y_{P_{\bf ac}X,+}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}).

By (
2.71
), we get

(2.100)

\|\widetilde{U}^{0}_{\gamma,t}Y_{P_{\bf ac}X,+}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}=\|P_{\bf ac}X\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}

and

\lim_{b\to+\infty}\limsup_{t\to+\infty}\|\chi_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\setminus\Delta_{b,t}}\widetilde{U}^{0}_{\gamma,t}Y_{P_{\bf ac}X,+}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}=0

follows from
(a)
. Hence, we get
(c)
.

Then, (
2.71
) implies, in particular, that the map
\mathcal{Y}_{+}\colon X\mapsto Y_{X,+}
is the unitary map from
H_{\bf ac}
onto
L^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{0})
. So,
\lim_{t\to+\infty}U_{t}^{-1}\widetilde{U}^{0}_{\gamma,t}Y
exists for every
Y\in L^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{0})
and
\lim_{t\to+\infty}U_{t}^{-1}\widetilde{U}^{0}_{\gamma,t}Y=\mathcal{Y}_{+}^{-1}Y
.

Summarizing, we have proved that the strong wave operator
W_{+}
in (
2.97
) exists and
W_{+}Y_{X,+}=P_{\bf ac}X
for every
X
, where
Y_{X,+}
is defined as in Theorem
2.19
. Analogously, one can check the existence of the wave operator
W_{-}
and prove the formula
W_{-}Y_{X,-}=P_{\bf ac}X
. All other assertions of the theorem are simple consequences of these two facts. ∎

The following corollary implies, in particular, that if Szegő measure
\mu
is purely a.c., then every
X\in H
propagates and the global
L^{2}(\mathcal{H})
asymptotics holds for
U_{t}X
. The reader can compare it to Theorem
2.19
which establishes the asymptotics over the finite interval.

Corollary 2.23
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hamiltonian of class
\mathbf{Sz}{\rm(CS)}
. Then, there exists a phase function
\gamma
such that the following assertion holds. For every
X\in H_{\bf ac}
, there are uniquely defined
Y_{X,\pm}\in L^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{0})
such that

(2.101)

\lim_{t\to\pm\infty}\|U_{t}X-\widetilde{U}^{0}_{\gamma,t}Y_{X,\pm}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}=0,

Moreover, if
\mathcal{H}
is diagonal, then one can take
\gamma=1
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

Proof.
Indeed, if
X\in H_{\bf ac}
, then

X=P_{\bf ac}X
, and (
2.101
) is equivalent to

\lim_{t\to\pm\infty}\|P_{\bf ac}X-U_{t}^{-1}\widetilde{U}^{0}_{\gamma,t}Y_{X,\pm}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}=0,

that holds by Theorem
2.22
. ∎

2.7.
Dynamical classification of spectral types

Our analysis allows to detect the spectral types of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{H}}
by observing the long-time dynamics of
U_{t}
.

Suppose
X\in H
is given. Denote the orthogonal projections to absolutely continuous, singular continuous, and pure point subspaces of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{H}}
by
P_{\bf ac},P_{\bf sc},
and
P_{\bf pp}
, respectively.
Our next result gives the dynamical characterization of whether
X
has nontrivial projections to any of these subspaces.

Theorem 2.24
.

Let
\mathcal{H}\in\mathbf{Sz}{\rm(CS)}
. Then, for every
X\in H
we have

(2.102)

\displaystyle\lim_{b\to+\infty}\lim_{\mathbf{T}\to+\infty}\frac{1}{\mathbf{T}}\int_{0}^{\mathbf{T}}\|U_{t}X\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},[0,L_{\mathcal{H}}(b)])}^{2}\,dt

\displaystyle=\|P_{\bf pp}X\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}^{2},

(2.103)

\displaystyle\lim_{b\to+\infty}\lim_{\mathbf{T}\to+\infty}\frac{1}{\mathbf{T}}\int_{0}^{\mathbf{T}}\|U_{t}X\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},[L_{\mathcal{H}}(b),L_{\mathcal{H}}(t-b)])}^{2}\,dt

\displaystyle=\|P_{\bf sc}X\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}^{2},

(2.104)

\displaystyle\lim_{b\to+\infty}\lim_{t\to+\infty}\|U_{t}X\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},[L_{\mathcal{H}}(t-b),L_{\mathcal{H}}(t+b)])}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|P_{\bf ac}X\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}^{2},

(2.105)

\displaystyle\lim_{b\to+\infty}\lim_{t\to+\infty}\|U_{t}X\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},[L_{\mathcal{H}}(t+b),+\infty))}

\displaystyle=0.

The analogous statements hold when
t\to+\infty
is replaced by
t\to-\infty
.

Proof.
We start with proving (
2.105
). Given
X
and
\varepsilon>0
, we can find
X_{\varepsilon}\in H_{c}
such that
\|X-X_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}\leqslant\varepsilon
. From Theorem
2.1
, we get

\lim_{b\to+\infty}\limsup_{t\to+\infty}\|U_{t}X_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},[L_{\mathcal{H}}(b+t),+\infty))}=0.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\limsup_{b\to+\infty}\limsup_{t\to+\infty}\|U_{t}X\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},[L_{\mathcal{H}}(b+t),+\infty))}\leqslant

\displaystyle\limsup_{b\to+\infty}\limsup_{t\to+\infty}\|U_{t}(X-X_{\varepsilon})\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},[L_{\mathcal{H}}(b+t),+\infty))}

\displaystyle+\limsup_{b\to+\infty}\limsup_{t\to+\infty}\|U_{t}X_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},[L_{\mathcal{H}}(b+t),+\infty))}\leqslant\varepsilon,

because
\|U_{t}(X-X_{\varepsilon})\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}=\|X-X_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}\leqslant\varepsilon
. Since
\varepsilon
is arbitrary, we get (
2.105
).

Formula (
2.104
) is immediate from Theorem
2.19
.

To prove (
2.102
), we apply Lemma
5.3
from Section
5.5
. Take
\Lambda>0
and
b\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\setminus\bigcup_{I\in\mathfrak{I}(\mathcal{H})}I.
Let
P_{[-\Lambda,\Lambda]}
be the orthogonal projection associated with the spectral decomposition of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{H}}
. We claim that the operator

\chi_{[0,b]}P_{[-\Lambda,\Lambda]}
is compact in
H
. Indeed, this follows from the formula

\chi_{[0,b]}(\tau)(P_{[-\Lambda,\Lambda]}X)(\tau)=\frac{\chi_{[0,b]}(\tau)}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{[-\Lambda,\Lambda]}\Theta(\tau,x)(\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}}X)(x)\,d\mu(x)

and the fact that the set
\{\int_{[-\Lambda,\Lambda]}\Theta(\tau,x)(\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}}X)\,d\mu\colon\|X\|_{H}\leqslant 1\}
is precompact in
C[0,b]
by Arzela-Ascoli theorem. Hence, by Lemma
5.3
applied to Hilbert space
H
, operator
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{H}}
, and
A=\chi_{[0,b]}
, one has

\lim_{\mathbf{T}\to\infty}\frac{1}{\mathbf{T}}\int_{0}^{\mathbf{T}}\|\chi_{[0,b]}U_{t}P_{[-\Lambda,\Lambda]}X\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}^{2}dt=\sum_{j}\|\chi_{[0,b]}P_{\{E_{j}\}}P_{[-\Lambda,\Lambda]}X\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})},

where
P_{\{E_{j}\}}
is orthogonal projection corresponding to eigenvalue
E_{j}
of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{H}}
and the sum is done over all eigenvalues.
Taking
b
to infinity (see Corollary 2 in
[
72
]
), we have

(2.106)

\lim_{b\to\infty}\lim_{\mathbf{T}\to\infty}\frac{1}{\mathbf{T}}\int_{0}^{\mathbf{T}}\|\chi_{[0,b]}U_{t}P_{[-\Lambda,\Lambda]}X\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}^{2}dt=\|P_{\bf pp}P_{[-\Lambda,\Lambda]}X\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})},

for every
X
. Now, taking
\Lambda\to\infty
, we get (
2.102
).

We are left with showing (
2.103
). Fix any
X
and
b>0
. Then,

\displaystyle\|U_{t}X\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}=

\displaystyle\|U_{t}X\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},[0,L_{\mathcal{H}}(b)])}+\|U_{t}X\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},[L_{\mathcal{H}}(b),L_{\mathcal{H}}(t-b)])}

\displaystyle+\|U_{t}X\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},[L_{\mathcal{H}}(t-b),L_{\mathcal{H}}(t+b)])}+\|U_{t}X\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},[L_{\mathcal{H}}(t+b),\infty))}.

We also have

\|U_{t}X\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}=\|X\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}=\|P_{\bf ac}X\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}+\|P_{\bf sc}X\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}+\|P_{\bf pp}X\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}.

Subtracting one identity from the other and taking the Cesaro mean, we get

\lim_{b\to\infty}\lim_{\mathbf{T}\to\infty}\left(\frac{1}{\mathbf{T}}\int_{0}^{\mathbf{T}}\|U_{t}X\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},[L_{\mathcal{H}}(b),L_{\mathcal{H}}(t-b)])}dt-\|P_{\bf sc}X\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}\right)=0,

as follows from already established (
2.102
), (
2.104
), and (
2.105
). The arguments for
t\to-\infty
are identical.
∎

We will need the following technical lemma later in the text.

Lemma 2.25
.

Suppose
\mathcal{H}
is a proper Hamiltonian,
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\setminus\bigcup_{I\in\mathfrak{I}(\mathcal{H})}I
,
X\in H_{c}
is supported on
[0,r]
, and
(X,\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right))_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}\neq 0
. If
Y
is defined by

\displaystyle Y(\tau)=0,\quad\tau>r,

(2.107)

\displaystyle Y(\tau)=J\int_{\tau}^{r}\mathcal{H}(s)X(s)ds,\quad\tau<r,

then
X
and
P_{H}Y
satisfy
|(\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}}X)(x)|^{2}+|(\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}}P_{H}Y)(x)|^{2}>0
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Such
X
and
Y
exist.

Proof.
First, observe that

\langle Y(0),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}=\langle J\int_{0}^{r}\mathcal{H}(s)X(s)ds,\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}=(X,\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right))_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}\neq 0.

Second, notice that
\Theta(\tau,0)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
and so
(\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}}X)(0)\neq 0
given assumptions of the lemma.
Suppose
x
is such that
x\neq 0
and
(\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}}X)(x)=0
. Observe that, by Lemma
2.18
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{H}}Y=\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}}P_{H}Y
. Then,

\displaystyle\sqrt{\pi}\cdot\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{H}}Y

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{r}\langle\mathcal{H}Y,\Theta(\tau,x)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}d\tau=-x^{-1}\int_{0}^{r}\langle JY,\Theta^{\prime}(\tau,x)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}d\tau

\displaystyle=x^{-1}\left(-\langle Y(0),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}+\int_{0}^{r}\langle JY^{\prime},\Theta(\tau,x)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}d\tau\right)

\displaystyle=x^{-1}\left(-\langle Y(0),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}+\int_{0}^{r}\langle\mathcal{H}(\tau)X(\tau),\Theta(\tau,x)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}d\tau\right)

\displaystyle=x^{-1}\left(-\langle Y(0),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}+\sqrt{\pi}(\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}}X)(x)\right)\neq 0,

where we used (
2.107
) and our other assumptions. Finally, since
\mathcal{H}
is proper, we can always find
X
that satisfies all conditions and define
Y
accordingly. For example,

X=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\cdot\chi_{[0,r]},\quad Y=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-\int_{\tau}^{r}h(s)ds\\
\int_{\tau}^{r}h_{1}(s)ds\end{smallmatrix}\right)\cdot\chi_{[0,r]}

is one possible choice.
∎

Theorem
2.24
gives a dynamical description of spectral types for each element
X
but it does not tell how to detect the presence of pure point, singular continuous, and absolutely continuous spectral types for
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{H}}
itself. We will address it in the next theorem. Recall that
\Delta_{b,t}
is defined as
\Delta_{b,t}=\bigl[L_{\mathcal{H}}(|t|-b),L_{\mathcal{H}}(|t|+b)\bigr]
for
b>0
.

Theorem 2.26
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hamiltonian of class
\mathbf{Sz}{\rm(CS)}
. Then, the following holds true.

(A)

If the singular spectrum of
\mathcal{H}
is empty, then

(2.108)

\lim_{b\to+\infty}\lim_{t\to+\infty}\|U_{t}X\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},\Delta_{b,t})}=\|X\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}

for every
X\in H
.

(B)

If there is some
X\in H_{c}
for which

(2.109)

\lim_{b\to+\infty}\lim_{\mathbf{T}\to+\infty}\frac{1}{\mathbf{T}}\int_{0}^{\mathbf{T}}\|U_{t}X\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},[L_{\mathcal{H}}(b),L_{\mathcal{H}}(t-b)])}^{2}\,dt=0,

then
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{H}}
has no singular continuous spectrum.

(C)

Let vectors
X
and
Y
be defined as in Lemma
2.25
. If both of the equalities

(2.110)

\displaystyle\lim_{b\to+\infty}\liminf_{t\to+\infty}\|U_{t}X\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},\Delta_{b,t})}

\displaystyle=\|X\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})},

(2.111)

\displaystyle\lim_{b\to+\infty}\liminf_{t\to+\infty}\|U_{t}P_{H}Y\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},\Delta_{b,t})}

\displaystyle=\|Y\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})},

hold, then the singular spectrum of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{H}}
is empty.

(D)

Let vectors
X
and
Y
be defined as in Lemma
2.25
. If both of the equalities

(2.112)

\displaystyle\lim_{b\to+\infty}\lim_{\mathbf{T}\to+\infty}\frac{1}{\mathbf{T}}\int_{0}^{\mathbf{T}}\|U_{t}X\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},[0,L_{\mathcal{H}}(b)])}^{2}\,dt

\displaystyle=0,

(2.113)

\displaystyle\lim_{b\to+\infty}\lim_{\mathbf{T}\to+\infty}\frac{1}{\mathbf{T}}\int_{0}^{\mathbf{T}}\|U_{t}Y\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},[0,L_{\mathcal{H}}(b)])}^{2}\,dt

\displaystyle=0,

hold, then
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{H}}
has no bound states.

We get the same conclusions if the limits
t\to+\infty
