are replaced by
t\to-\infty
.

Proof.
Suppose the singular spectrum is empty, then
X=P_{\bf ac}X
and our claim follows from (
2.105
).

Then, suppose
X\in H_{c}
is such that (
2.109
) holds. Recalling Theorem
2.19
, consider
f=\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}}X
.
Represent the measure
\mu=\mu_{\bf ac}+\mu_{\bf s}
as a sum of absolutely continuous and singular components and further write
\mu_{\bf s}=\mu_{\bf sc}+\mu_{\bf pp}
as a sum of singular continuous and pure point parts. Then,
(
2.103
) gives

\int_{\mathbb{R}}|f|^{2}\,d\mu_{\bf sc}=0.

On the other hand,
f
is an entire function that can have only countably many zeroes in
\mathbb{C}
. Therefore,
|f|>0
a.e. with respect to
\mu_{\bf sc}
and so
\mu_{\bf sc}=0
.

To show
(C)
, we only need to prove that (
2.110
), (
2.111
) imply that the spectrum of
\mathcal{H}
is purely absolutely continuous. If
f:=\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}}X
and
g:=\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}}P_{H}Y
, then (
2.110
) and (
2.111
) give

\int_{\mathbb{R}}(|f|^{2}+|g|^{2})\,d\mu_{\bf s}=0.

That, however, contradicts Lemma
2.25
unless
\mu_{\bf s}(\mathbb{R})=0
.

Finally, to get
(D)
, we notice that (
2.112
), (
2.113
) and (
2.102
) give
P_{\bf pp}X=P_{\bf pp}Y=0
which can be rewritten as

\int_{\mathbb{R}}(|f|^{2}+|g|^{2})\,d\mu_{\bf pp}=0,

where, again,
f=\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}}X
and
g=\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}}P_{H}Y
. Since
f
and
g
are entire functions that have no common zeroes by Lemma
2.25
, we get
\mu_{\bf pp}=0
.

The arguments for
t\to-\infty
are identical. ∎

3.
Krein strings

The theory of Krein strings goes back to works by M. Krein
[
53
]
and Feller
[
31
]
. In this section, we recall some basic definitions and facts, explain the connection between Krein strings and diagonal canonical systems, and use it to translate some results obtained in the previous section to the new setting.

3.1.
Krein strings

Let
0<L\leqslant\infty
and
M
be a non-decreasing right-continuous function on
(-\infty,L)
, satisfying
M(\xi)=0,\xi<0
. The Lebesgue-Stieltjes measure
\mathfrak{m}
on
[0,L)
is defined by
\mathfrak{m}[0,\xi]=M(\xi)
. We write its decomposition into the absolutely continuous and singular parts as
\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{m}_{\bf ac}+\mathfrak{m}_{\bf s}=\rho(\xi)\,d\xi+\mathfrak{m}_{\bf s}
. Recall that in our notation
M(L-)=\lim_{\xi\uparrow L}M(\xi)
and we call the
[M,L]
pair
proper
if
M
and
L
satisfy the following conditions

(3.1)

\displaystyle L+M(L-)=\infty,

(3.2)

\displaystyle 0<M(\xi)<M(L-),\quad\forall\xi\in(0,L).

These two conditions are very natural from the point of view of spectral theory
[
40
]
. They guarantee that the spectral measure
\sigma
of the string operator is unique in the class of spectral measures with non-negative support. Additionally, they make sure that the map
[M,L]\mapsto\sigma
is injective.
In this paper, we will work with proper
[M,L]
pairs only. Let us consider functions
\varphi
,
\psi
defined by the integral equations

\varphi(\xi,z)=1-z\int_{[0,\xi]}(\xi-s)\varphi(s,z)\,d\mathfrak{m}(s),

\psi(\xi,z)=\xi-z\int_{[0,\xi]}(\xi-s)\psi(s,z)\,d\mathfrak{m}(s),

where
\xi\in[0,L)
,
z\in\mathbb{C}
.
It is customary to extend
\varphi
and
\psi
to
(-\infty,0)
by
\varphi(\xi,z)=1
and
\psi(\xi,z)=\xi
where
\xi<0
.
These functions are uniquely determined by the string
[M,L]
and they define the Titchmarsh-Weyl function
q
of
[M,L]
by

(3.3)

q(z)=\lim_{\xi\to L}\frac{\psi(\xi,z)}{\varphi(\xi,z)},\qquad z\in\mathbb{C}\backslash[0,\infty),

see formula (2.21) in
[
41
]
or Theorem 10.1 in
[
40
]
. That function
q
has the unique integral representation

(3.4)

q(z)=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\frac{d\sigma(\lambda)}{\lambda-z},\qquad z\in\mathbb{C}\backslash[0,\infty),

where
\sigma
, the
main (or orthogonal) spectral measure
of the string
[M,L]
, is a nonnegative Borel measure on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
satisfying condition

\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\frac{d\sigma(\lambda)}{1+\lambda}<\infty.

We emphasize (see
[
40
]
) that a proper string is in the limit-point case if and only if

(3.5)

\int_{[0,L)}\xi^{2}d\mathfrak{m}=+\infty.

However, when the integral in (
3.5
) is finite and we are in limit-circle case, the main spectral measure with
non-negative
support is unique and is given by (
3.3
). Later in the text, we will focus on strings
[M,L]
in Szegő class. For this type of strings, the condition (
3.5
) is always satisfied.

Similarly to (
2.7
), one can define the generalized Fourier transform associated with the string
[M,L]
:

(3.6)

{\mathcal{U}}_{M}\colon v\mapsto\int_{0}^{L}v(\xi)\varphi(\xi,z)\,d\mathfrak{m}(\xi),\qquad z\in\mathbb{C},

starting with functions
v\in L_{c}^{2}(\mathfrak{m})
that have compact support in
[0,L)
. It is known (see Section 10 in
[
40
]
) that
{\mathcal{U}}_{M}
can be extended to the unitary operator from
L^{2}(\mathfrak{m})
onto
L^{2}(\sigma)
.
The inverse map is given by (see formula (2.25) in
[
41
]
)

v={\mathcal{U}}_{M}^{-1}({\mathcal{U}}_{M}v)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\varphi(\xi,\lambda)({\mathcal{U}}_{M}v)(\lambda)\,d\sigma(\lambda),\qquad\xi\in[0,L),

where the last integral can be first densely defined on
L_{c}^{2}(\sigma)
and then extended to all of
L^{2}(\sigma)
. Let us define the Krein string operator
\mathcal{S}_{M}
by

\mathcal{S}_{M}={\mathcal{U}}_{M}^{-1}M_{\lambda}{\mathcal{U}}_{M},

where
\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathcal{S}_{M}:=\{v\in L^{2}(\mathfrak{m}):M_{\lambda}{\mathcal{U}}_{M}v\in L^{2}(\sigma)
} and we recall that
M_{\lambda}f
is a function in
\lambda
which is equal to
\lambda f(\lambda)
. Clearly,
\mathcal{S}_{M}
is a self-adjoint operator in
L^{2}(\mathfrak{m})
.

3.2.
Connection between Krein strings and canonical systems with diagonal Hamiltonians

Suppose
[M,L]
is a proper string.
Consider the increasing function
N\colon\xi\mapsto\xi+M(\xi)
on
[0,L)
and let
\mathfrak{n}
denote the corresponding measure,
\mathfrak{n}[0,\xi]=N(\xi)
for
\xi\in[0,L)
. Condition (
3.1
) is equivalent to
N(L-)=+\infty
. Define the function
N^{(-1)}
as generalized inverse of
N
, see Section
1.4
. Using the fact that
N
is strictly increasing, one can show that
N^{(-1)}
is continuous on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, and we have
N^{(-1)}(N(\xi))=\xi
for every
\xi\in[0,L)
. Recall that
\rho
is the density of the absolutely continuous part of
\mathfrak{m}
, so that
\mathfrak{m}=\rho\,d\xi+\mathfrak{m}_{s}
. Define two functions on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
:

(3.7)

\alpha(\tau)=\begin{cases}1,&\mbox{if }N^{(-1)}(\tau)\in\Omega_{\bf s}(\mathfrak{m}),\\
\frac{\rho(N^{(-1)}(\tau))}{1+\rho(N^{(-1)}(\tau))},&\mbox{otherwise}.\end{cases}

and

(3.8)

\beta(\tau)=\begin{cases}0,&\mbox{if }N^{(-1)}(\tau)\in\Omega_{\bf s}(\mathfrak{m}),\\
\frac{1}{1+\rho(N^{(-1)}(\tau))},&\mbox{otherwise},\end{cases}

Given
\alpha,\beta
, define
\mathcal{H}_{\ast}=\mathrm{diag}(\alpha,\beta)
.
If
\mathcal{H}_{\ast}
is proper, we let

(3.9)

\xi(\tau)=\int_{0}^{\tau}\beta(s)ds,\quad L=\int_{0}^{\infty}\beta(s)ds,\quad M(\xi)=\int_{0}^{\sup\{x\colon\xi(x)=\xi\}}\alpha(s)ds,\quad\xi<L.

We now collect some facts related to the well-known connection between Krein strings and diagonal canonical systems. The first of them can be found in
[
34
]
(see Section 8 in Chapter 6), p. 239 in
[
28
]
, or
[
41
]
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Formulas (
3.7
), (
3.8
), and (
3.9
) establish the bijection
[M,L]\mapsto\mathrm{diag}(\alpha,\beta)
between proper
[M,L]
pairs and proper Hamiltonians
\mathcal{H}_{\ast}=\mathrm{diag}(\alpha,\beta)
with unit trace.

We want to make one comment here. The references
[
28
]
,
[
34
]
, an
[
41
]
explain that connection for the general strings and diagonal Hamiltonians. However, one can see that the proper strings correspond to proper Hamiltonians. Indeed, the assumption that the left end of the string is heavy is equivalent to the condition that the Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}_{\ast}=\mathrm{diag}(\alpha,\beta)
is not equal to
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0\\
0&1\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)
on
[0,\varepsilon]
with some
\varepsilon>0
. Moreover,
making the assumption that

L+M(L-)=+\infty
and the right end is heavy is equivalent to saying that
\mathcal{H}_{\ast}
is not equal to either
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0\\
0&1\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)
or
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
on
(\tau_{0},\infty)
for some
\tau_{0}>0
.

For the proof of the following result, check Theorem 4.2 in
[
41
]
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
[M,L]
and
\mathcal{H}_{*}
be the string and its corresponding Hamiltonian obtained via the bijection in Lemma
3.1
.
Then, for the corresponding Titchmarsh-Weyl functions
q
,
m_{*}
, we have

(3.10)

zq(z^{2})=m_{\ast}(z),\quad z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}.

Consequently, the spectral measures
\sigma
,
\mu_{\ast}
of
[M,L]
,
\mathcal{H}_{*}
satisfy

(3.11)

\mu_{\ast}([E_{1},E_{2}])=\frac{\pi}{2}\sigma([E_{1}^{2},E_{2}^{2}]),\quad\mu_{\ast}(\{0\})=\pi\sigma(\{0\})

for all
0<E_{1}<E_{2}
.

Relation (
3.11
) shows that the operators
\mathcal{D}^{2}_{\mathcal{H}_{\ast}}
and
\mathcal{S}_{M}
are unitarily equivalent. The unitary equivalence is given via the explicit operator
\Upsilon
in the lemma below.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
[M,L]
and
\mathcal{H}_{*}
be the string and its corresponding Hamiltonian obtained via the bijection in Lemma
3.1
. Then, the map
\Upsilon\colon v\in L^{2}(\mathfrak{m})\mapsto X=(v\circ N^{(-1)},0)^{t}
is a unitary map onto the subspace
\{X=(X_{1},X_{2})^{t}\in H:X_{2}=0\}
of the space
L^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{\ast})
. Moreover,

(3.12)

\Upsilon^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{2}_{\mathcal{H}_{\ast}}\Upsilon=\mathcal{S}_{M}.

Let us give a sketch of the proof of this well-known fact. The map
\Upsilon
is correctly defined and unitary due to (
3.7
), (
3.8
) and the change of variables in the Lebesgue-Stieltjes integral:

\int_{0}^{L}|v(\xi)|^{2}d\mathfrak{m}=\int_{0}^{\infty}|v(N^{(-1)}(\tau))|^{2}\alpha(\tau)d\tau.

To prove (
3.12
), it is convenient to work on the spectral side of both
\mathcal{S}_{M}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{H}_{\ast}}
.
We will check that
\mathcal{D}^{2}_{\mathcal{H}_{\ast}}\Upsilon\mathcal{U}_{M}^{-1}g=\Upsilon\mathcal{S}_{M}\mathcal{U}_{M}^{-1}g
for every
g\in L^{2}(\sigma)
such that
\lambda g\in L^{2}(\sigma)
, where
\sigma
is the main spectral measure of
[M,L]
. The monodromy matrix of
\mathcal{H}_{*}
has the form

(3.13)

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\Theta_{\ast}^{+}(\tau,z)&\Phi_{\ast}^{+}(\tau,z)\\
\Theta_{\ast}^{-}(\tau,z)&\Phi_{\ast}^{-}(\tau,z)\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\varphi(\xi,z^{2})&z\psi(\xi,z^{2})\\
z^{-1}\varphi^{\prime}(\xi-,z^{2})&\psi^{\prime}(\xi-,z^{2})\end{smallmatrix}\right),\qquad\xi=N^{(-1)}(\tau),

for details, see, e.g., Lemma 4.1 in
[
41
]
. Then, we obtain

\displaystyle(\Upsilon(\mathcal{U}_{M}^{-1}g))(\tau)

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}g(\lambda)\varphi(N^{(-1)}(\tau),\lambda)\,d\sigma,0\right)^{t}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(x^{2})\Theta^{+}_{\ast}(\tau,x)\,d\mu_{\ast},0\right)^{t}=\mathcal{W}^{-1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\ast}}(\pi^{-\frac{1}{2}}g(x^{2})),

using the fact that
g(x^{2})
is even, measure
\mu_{\ast}
in (
3.11
) is even, and
\Theta_{\ast}^{-}(\tau,x)
is odd in
x
.
We see that

(3.14)

(\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}_{\ast}}\Upsilon\,\,\mathcal{U}_{M}^{-1}g)(x)=\frac{g(x^{2})}{\sqrt{\pi}}.

Notice that, thanks to (
3.11
), the map
g(\lambda)\mapsto g(\lambda^{2})/\sqrt{\pi}
is a unitary map of
L^{2}(\sigma)
onto to the set of even functions in
L^{2}(\mu_{\ast})
. Hence,
\Upsilon\mathcal{U}_{M}^{-1}g
belongs to the domain of
\mathcal{D}^{2}_{\mathcal{H}_{\ast}}
if
g,\lambda g\in L^{2}(\sigma)
. Moreover, for
v=\mathcal{U}_{M}^{-1}g
we have

(3.15)

\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}_{\ast}}\mathcal{D}^{2}_{\mathcal{H}_{\ast}}\Upsilon v=(\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}_{\ast}}\mathcal{D}^{2}_{\mathcal{H}_{\ast}}\mathcal{W}^{-1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\ast}})\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}_{\ast}}\Upsilon v\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{lkj}}}{{=}}x^{2}\cdot\frac{g(x^{2})}{\sqrt{\pi}}.

On the other hand, (
3.14
) also yields

(3.16)

\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}_{\ast}}\Upsilon\,\,\mathcal{S}_{M}v=x^{2}\cdot\frac{g(x^{2})}{\sqrt{\pi}}.

The comparison of (
3.15
) and (
3.16
) now gives
\mathcal{D}^{2}_{\mathcal{H}_{\ast}}\Upsilon\mathcal{U}_{M}^{-1}g=\Upsilon\mathcal{S}_{M}\mathcal{U}_{M}^{-1}g
, as desired.

3.3.
Wave equation for Krein strings

The vibration of the proper string with parameters
[M,L]
is governed by the following formal hyperbolic Cauchy problem:

(3.17)

u_{tt}+\mathcal{S}_{M}u=0,\quad u(\xi,0)=u_{0}(\xi),\quad u_{t}(\xi,0)=0,\quad u^{\prime}(0-,t)=0,

where
u_{0}
is the initial displacement of the string, its initial velocity is equal to zero, and the Neumann boundary condition
u^{\prime}(0-,t)=0
indicates that its left end is “loose”. In this paper, we will only study solutions to (
3.17
) given by the formula

(3.18)

u(\xi,t)=\cos(t\sqrt{\mathcal{S}_{M}})u_{0},\quad u_{0}\in L^{2}(\mathfrak{m}),

where
\cos(t\sqrt{\mathcal{S}_{M}})
is defined via spectral theorem:

\cos(t\sqrt{\mathcal{S}_{M}})u_{0}=\mathcal{U}_{M}^{-1}\Bigl(\cos(t\sqrt{\lambda})(\mathcal{U}_{M}u_{0})(\lambda)\Bigr).

Function
u
is understood as element in
C(\mathbb{R},L^{2}(\mathfrak{m}))\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},L^{2}(\mathfrak{m}))
in the standard mixed-norm notation. Let us notice that assumption
u_{0}\in{\rm dom}\,\mathcal{S}_{M}
implies that
u
is
strong solution
which means that it is twice strongly continuously differentiable function of
t
in the topology of Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathfrak{m})
and that it satisfies equation for every
t\geqslant 0
and initial conditions for
t=0
(see
[
5
]
, p. 225). The uniqueness of such strong solution follows immediately from the self-adjointness of
\mathcal{S}_{M}
(see
[
5
]
, Theorem 6.2 on p. 229). Assumption
u_{0}\in{\rm dom}\,\mathcal{S}_{M}
for real-valued
u_{0}
guarantees that the energy is finite since

\displaystyle E(t)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{[0,L)}(u_{t}^{2}+(\mathcal{S}_{M}u)u)\,d\mathfrak{m}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda(\cos^{2}(t\sqrt{\lambda})+\sin^{2}(t\sqrt{\lambda}))\cdot(\mathcal{U}_{M}u_{0})^{2}d\sigma=

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\|\sqrt{\mathcal{S}_{M}}u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathfrak{m})}^{2}<\infty.

Thus, one can argue that real initial data
u_{0}\in{\rm dom}\,\mathcal{S}_{M}
give rise to solutions that make physical sense. Since
u
is a linear operator of
u_{0}
, we can assume that
u_{0}
is real when studying the dynamics of
u
.

Using spectral theorem, formula (
3.18
) can be rewritten as follows. Let
u
be defined by (
3.18
) and
U_{t}
be an evolution for the canonical system with the Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}_{*}=\mathrm{diag}(\alpha,\beta)
in which
\alpha
and
\beta
are obtained by formulas (
3.7
) and (
3.8
). Then,

(3.19)

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}u(\xi,t)\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\Bigl((U_{t}X)(\tau)+(U_{-t}X)(\tau)\Bigr),

where
X(\tau)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}u_{0}(N^{(-1)}(\tau))\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right),\,\xi=N^{(-1)}(\tau),\,\tau\geqslant 0
.

Proof of Theorem
1.7
.

A change of variables in Lebesgue-Stieltjes integral gives

(3.20)

T_{M}(\xi)=\int_{0}^{\tau}\sqrt{\alpha(l)\beta(l)}dl=T_{\mathcal{H}_{*}}(\tau),\quad\xi=N^{(-1)}(\tau),

if the string
[M,L]
and the Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}_{*}
are related as in Lemma
3.1
. The proof is now immediate from (
3.19
), (
3.20
), and Theorem
2.1
.
∎

Remark 3.4
.

If the number
L_{\mathcal{H}}(t+a)
in (
2.19
) is an endpoint of indivisible interval, it must be its left endpoint by definition. Hence, Theorem
2.1
and the formula for operator
\Upsilon
show that
\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathfrak{m},\{\mathfrak{fr}_{t}\})}=0
, i.e., the wavefront as a point never carries a positive
L^{2}(\mathfrak{m})
–norm of solution.

The Theorem
1.7
can be applied to many models. The vibration of the classical infinite Stieltjes string with beads of equal masses connected by massless wire exhibits infinite speed of propagation (see, e.g.,
[
81
]
, p.25) and that example corresponds to
\rho=0
and
\mathfrak{m}_{\bf s}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\delta(\xi-j)
where
\delta(\xi)
denotes the unit point-mass at zero. The formula (
1.19
) for the front of the wave confirms our intuition that the wave propagates instantaneously through the intervals on which
\rho=0
. In fact, it shows that the presence of nontrivial
\mathfrak{m}_{\bf s}
on such an interval plays no role in that phenomenon. For example, if
\rho=1
for
\xi\notin[\alpha,\beta],0<\alpha<\beta<\infty
and
\rho=0
on
[\alpha,\beta]
, then the formula (
1.19
) yields

\mathfrak{fr}_{t}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathfrak{fr}_{0}+t,&t\leqslant t_{\bf cr},\\
\beta+t,&t>t_{\bf cr}\end{array}\right.,\quad t_{\bf cr}=\alpha-\mathfrak{fr}_{0}

as along as
\mathfrak{fr}_{0}<\alpha
. Observe that we have
\mathfrak{fr}_{t_{\bf cr}}=\alpha
at the critical time
t_{\bf cr}
since
L_{M}
is left-continuous.

Recall that the measure

\sigma=\upsilon\,m+\sigma_{\bf s}
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
with the density
\upsilon
and the singular part
\sigma_{\bf s}
belongs to the Szegő class
\mathrm{Sz}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
if
(x+1)^{-1}\in L^{1}(\sigma)
and

\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\frac{\log\upsilon(x)}{\sqrt{x}(x+1)}\,dx>-\infty.

A simple change of variables shows that
\sigma\in\mathrm{Sz}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
if and only if
\mu_{\ast}\in\mathrm{Sz}(\mathbb{R})
, where
\mu_{\ast}
is taken from Lemma
3.2
. In the Introduction, the class of strings for which the spectral measure is Szegő was called
\mathbf{Sz}{\rm(Str)}
and it was characterized in Theorem
1.10
. The following result gives its dynamical description and has Theorem
1.8
as a corollary.

Theorem 3.5
.

Suppose
[M,L]
is a proper string. If there is
u_{0}\in L_{c}^{2}(\mathfrak{m})
such that

(3.21)

\limsup_{t\to+\infty}\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathfrak{m},\Delta_{\ell,a,t})}>0,\qquad\Delta_{\ell,a,t}:=\bigl[L_{M}(t+a-\ell),L_{M}(t+a)\bigr],

then
[M,L]\in\mathbf{Sz}{\rm(Str)}
. Here,
a=T_{M}(\mathfrak{fr}_{0})
. Conversely, if
[M,L]\in\mathbf{Sz}{\rm(Str)}
, then

(3.22)

\liminf_{t\to+\infty}\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathfrak{m},\Delta_{\ell,a,t})}>0

for every
u_{0}\in L_{c}^{2}(\mathfrak{m})
not equal to zero identically and for every
\ell>0
. Here, again,
a=T_{M}(\mathfrak{fr}_{0})
.

Proof.
That follows from Corollary
2.13
and (
3.19
).∎

Remark 3.6
.

Combined with the Theorem
3.7
below, we conclude that (
3.22
) can be strengthened to

\lim_{t\to+\infty}\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathfrak{m},\Delta_{\ell,a,t})}>0

for every
\ell>0
.

The solution to (
3.17
) for the homogeneous string
[M,L]=[d\xi,\infty]
is given explicitly via d’Alembert’s formula:

(3.23)

V_{t}^{0}u_{0}=\frac{u_{0}(\xi+t)+u_{0}(\xi-t)}{2},

where
u_{0}
is extended to
\mathbb{R}
as even function. Note that
V_{t}^{0}u_{0}=\frac{u_{0}(\xi-t)}{2}+o(1)
,
t\to\infty
, with
o(1)
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
. Hence, the evolution is equivalent to translation when
t\to+\infty
. For general strings, we need to introduce the modified dynamics. Given
y\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, we let

(3.24)

\widetilde{V}_{t}^{0}y\colon\xi\mapsto\frac{1}{2}\chi_{\Omega_{\bf ac}(\mathfrak{m})}(\xi)\cdot\rho^{-\frac{1}{4}}(\xi)\cdot y(T_{M}(\xi)-t),\qquad\xi\in{[0,L)}.

A change of variables gives
\|\widetilde{V}_{t}^{0}y\|_{L^{2}(\mathfrak{m})}\leqslant\|y\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}
.
If
\sigma\in\mathrm{Sz}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
and
\sigma=\upsilon\,dx+\sigma_{\bf s}
, then its Szegő function is defined by

D_{\sigma}(z):=\frac{{D}_{\mu_{\ast}}(\sqrt{z})}{\sqrt{\pi}\sqrt[4]{z}},\qquad z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}_{+},

where the measure
\mu_{\ast}
, given by (
3.11
), is the spectral measure of Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}_{\ast}
. Notice that
|D_{\sigma}(\lambda)|^{2}=\upsilon(\lambda)
for a.e.
\lambda>0
in the sense of non-tangential boundary values.
In the case when
[M,L]\in\mathbf{Sz}{\rm(Str)}
, we can obtain the asymptotics of
u
near its wavefront. The following result implies Theorem
1.9
from the Introduction.

Theorem 3.7
.

Suppose
[M,L]\in\mathbf{Sz}{\rm(Str)}
. Then, there is a map
u_{0}\mapsto G_{u_{0}}
from
L^{2}(\mathfrak{m})
to
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, such that for every fixed positive
a
we have

(3.25)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to+\infty}\|u-\widetilde{V}_{t}^{0}G_{u_{0}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathfrak{m},[L_{M}(t-a),L_{M}(t+a)])}=0.

The function
G_{u_{0}}
satisfies
\|G_{u_{0}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}=2\|u_{0,\bf ac}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathfrak{m})}
, where
u_{0,\bf ac}
is the orthogonal projection of
u_{0}
to the absolutely continuous subspace
H_{\bf ac}(\mathcal{S}_{M})
of
\mathcal{S}_{M}
. Moreover, if
u_{0}\in H_{\bf ac}(\mathcal{S}_{M})
, then

(3.26)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to+\infty}\|u-\widetilde{V}_{t}^{0}G_{u_{0}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathfrak{m})}=0.

Proof.
We will prove that (
3.25
) and (
3.26
) hold with the following choice of the function
G_{u_{0}}
:

(3.27)

G_{u_{0}}(\eta)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\frac{\mathop{\rm Re}(D_{\sigma}(\alpha)e^{i\eta\sqrt{\alpha}})g_{\bf ac}(\alpha)}{\sqrt[4]{\alpha}}\,d\alpha,\quad g:=\mathcal{U}_{M}u_{0},\quad g_{\bf ac}:=g\cdot\chi_{\Omega_{\bf ac}(\sigma)}\,.

Fix
a>0
and choose
b>a
.
Define
\mathcal{H}_{\ast}
by formulas (
3.7
), (
3.8
) and note that
\mathcal{H}_{\ast}\in\mathbf{Sz}{\rm(CS)}
. Set
X=\Upsilon u_{0}
and let, as above,
\xi=N^{(-1)}(\tau)
for
\tau\geqslant 0
. Formula (
3.19
) and Theorem
2.19
give

(3.28)

\begin{pmatrix}u(\xi,t)\\
0\end{pmatrix}=\frac{1}{2}\Bigl((U_{t}X)(\tau)+(U_{-t}X)(\tau)\Bigr)=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{H}_{\ast,\mathfrak{n}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\tau)(U_{t}^{0}Y_{X,+}+U_{-t}^{0}Y_{X,-})(T_{\mathcal{H}_{*}}(\tau))+o(1),

as
t\to+\infty
, with
o(1)
in
L^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{\ast},\Delta_{b,t})
, where

\mathcal{H}_{\ast,\mathfrak{n}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\tau)=\begin{cases}\begin{pmatrix}\left(\frac{\beta(\tau)}{\alpha(\tau)}\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}&0\\
0&\left(\frac{\alpha(\tau)}{\beta(\tau)}\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}\end{pmatrix},&\tau\colon\;\beta(\tau)\alpha(\tau)>0,\\
0,&\tau\colon\;\beta(\tau)\alpha(\tau)=0,\end{cases}

is the matrix from (
2.68
), and
f=\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}_{\ast}}X
,
f_{\bf ac}=f\cdot\chi_{\Omega_{\bf ac}(\mu_{\ast})}
,
\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}Y_{X,+}=f_{\bf ac}\overline{D_{\mu_{\ast}}}
,
\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}Y_{X,-}=f_{\bf ac}D_{\mu_{\ast}}
. Applying (
2.74
), we obtain

\frac{1}{2}(U_{t}^{0}Y_{X,+}+U_{-t}^{0}Y_{X,-})(\tau)=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\left(\widehat{h}_{+}(\tau-t)\left(\begin{matrix}1\\
-i\end{matrix}\right)+\widecheck{h}_{-}(\tau-t)\left(\begin{matrix}1\\
{i}\end{matrix}\right)\right)+o(1),

as
t\to+\infty
, with
o(1)
in
L^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{0})
and
h_{+}=f_{\bf ac}\overline{D_{\mu_{\ast}}}
,
h_{-}=f_{\bf ac}D_{\mu_{\ast}}
. In other words, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(U_{t}^{0}Y_{X,+}+U_{-t}^{0}Y_{X,-})(\tau)=\left(\begin{matrix}A_{f}(\tau-t)\\
0\end{matrix}\right)+o(1),\qquad t\to+\infty,

where
o(1)
is in
L^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{0})
and

A_{f}(\eta)=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f_{\bf ac}(x)\mathop{\rm Re}(D_{\mu_{\ast}}(x)e^{ix\eta})\,dx,\qquad\eta\in\mathbb{R}.

The formula (
3.28
) implies

(3.29)

\begin{pmatrix}u(\xi,t)\\
0\end{pmatrix}=\mathcal{H}_{\ast,\mathfrak{n}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\tau)\left(\begin{matrix}A_{f}(T_{\mathcal{H}_{*}}(\tau)-t)\\
0\end{matrix}\right)+\mathcal{H}_{\ast,\mathfrak{n}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\tau)R_{1,t}(T_{\mathcal{H}_{*}}(\tau))+R_{2,t}(\tau),

where
\|R_{1,t}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{0})}+\|R_{2,t}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},\Delta_{b,t})}\to 0
as
t\to+\infty
. Note that considering
\xi\in\Omega_{\bf ac}(\mathfrak{m})
for which
\rho(\xi)>0
is the same as considering those
\tau\geqslant 0
for which
\det\mathcal{H}_{\ast}(\tau)=\alpha(\tau)\beta(\tau)>0
. Moreover, for such
\xi
and
\tau
we have

\rho^{-\frac{1}{4}}(\xi)=\left(\frac{\alpha(\tau)}{\beta(\tau)}\right)^{\frac{1}{4}},\qquad T_{M}(\xi)=T_{\mathcal{H}_{*}}(\tau).

So, one can rewrite relation (
3.29
) in the form

\begin{pmatrix}u(\xi,t)-\chi_{\Omega_{\bf ac}(\mathfrak{m})}(\xi)\rho^{-\frac{1}{4}}(\xi)A_{f}(T_{M}(\xi)-t)\\
0\end{pmatrix}=\mathcal{H}_{\ast,\mathfrak{n}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\tau)R_{1,t}(T_{\mathcal{H}_{*}}(\tau))+R_{2,t}(\tau),

or in the form

\Upsilon\bigl(u(\cdot,t)-\widetilde{V}_{t}^{0}y\bigr)(\tau)=\mathcal{H}_{\ast,\mathfrak{n}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\tau)R_{1,t}(T_{\mathcal{H}_{*}}(\tau))+R_{2,t}(\tau),

with
y=2A_{f}
. Since
b>a
, the mapping
\Upsilon
sends
L^{2}(\mathfrak{m},[L_{M}(t-a),L_{M}(t+a)])
into a subset of
L^{2}(\mathcal{H},\Delta_{b,t})
. Noting that

\|\mathcal{H}_{\ast,\mathfrak{n}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\cdot(R_{1,t}\circ T_{\mathcal{H}_{*}})+R_{2,t}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},\Delta_{b,t})}\leqslant\|R_{1,t}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{0})}+\|R_{2,t}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H},\Delta_{b,t})}\to 0,\qquad t\to+\infty,

we see that (
3.25
) holds with
G_{u_{0}}=y=2A_{f}
. If we put
g=\mathcal{U}_{M}u_{0}
, then

g
and
f
are related to each other by
f(x)=g(x^{2})/\sqrt{\pi}
according to (
3.14
).
By construction, the function
f
is even, and

D_{\mu_{\ast}}(-x)=\overline{D_{\mu_{\ast}}(x)}
almost everywhere on
\mathbb{R}
since
\mu_{\ast}
is even.
After changing variables
x^{2}=\alpha
, we obtain

\displaystyle G_{u_{0}}(\eta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f_{\bf ac}(x)\mathop{\rm Re}(D_{\mu_{\ast}}(x)e^{ix\eta})\,dx=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\frac{g_{\bf ac}(\alpha)\mathop{\rm Re}(D_{\sigma}(\alpha)e^{i\sqrt{\alpha}\eta})}{\sqrt[4]{\alpha}}\,d\alpha,

as in (
3.27
). We also have

\|G_{u_{0}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}=4\|A_{f}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}=2\|f_{\bf ac}\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{\ast})}^{2}=2\|P_{\bf ac}X\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}^{2}=2\|u_{0,\bf ac}\|_{L^{2}(\mathfrak{m})}^{2}=2\|g_{\bf ac}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\sigma)}
, where in the second identity we have used the formula

A_{f}(\eta)=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f_{\bf ac}(x)\mathop{\rm Re}(D_{\mu_{\ast}}(x)e^{ix\eta})\,dx=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f_{\bf ac}(x)D_{\mu_{\ast}}(x)e^{ix\eta}\,dx,

in which the integrals are understood in the
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
-sense. Finally, Corollary
2.23
implies (
3.26
).∎

3.4.
Examples

In this subsection, we explain how the general results can be applied to two examples considered in the Introduction.

Example
1.11
: strings for which
\rho=1
.

Consider the case when
L=\infty
and
\rho=1
. For the associated measure
\mathfrak{m}
, we get

(3.30)

d\mathfrak{m}=d\xi+d\mathfrak{m}_{\bf s},

where
\mathfrak{m}_{\bf s}
is any singular measure. If
\mathfrak{m}_{\bf s}=0
, then the solution
u
is given by (
3.23
). The models described by our choice of
M
are numerous, e.g., think about the beads with masses
\{m_{j}\}
placed at points
\{\xi_{j}\},\xi_{0}<\xi_{1}<\ldots
connected by the string with a uniform density equal to one.

One might want to know how the presence of “impurities” encoded by
\mathfrak{m}_{\bf s}
changes the character of wave propagation. The general results from the previous subsection can be reformulated as follows. From Theorem
1.7
, we immediately get

Proposition 3.8
.

If
u_{0}\in L_{c}^{2}(\mathfrak{m})
is nonzero, then
\mathfrak{fr}_{t}=\mathfrak{fr}_{0}+t
.

Clearly, the front propagates with the same linear speed regardless of the nature of
\mathfrak{m}_{\bf s}
. For
M
that satisfy
\rho=1
, it was established (see
[
6
]
), that

(3.31)

\mathfrak{m}_{\bf s}(\mathbb{R}_{+})<\infty\Longleftrightarrow\sigma\in\mathrm{Sz}(\mathbb{R}_{+}).

In the next two statements, we describe how the dynamics of
u
depends on
\mathfrak{m}_{\bf s}
.

Proposition 3.9
.

If
\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathfrak{m})}>0,\mathfrak{fr}_{0}<\infty
, and

\mathfrak{m}_{\bf s}(\mathbb{R}_{+})=\infty
, then

\lim_{t\to\infty}\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathfrak{m},[\mathfrak{fr}_{t}-a,\mathfrak{fr}_{t}])}=0

for every fixed
a>0
. Conversely, if there is
u_{0}
that satisfies
\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathfrak{m})}>0,\mathfrak{fr}_{0}<\infty
, and

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathfrak{m},[\mathfrak{fr}_{t}-a,\mathfrak{fr}_{t}])}>0

for some fixed
a>0
, then
\mathfrak{m}_{\bf s}(\mathbb{R}_{+})<\infty
.

Proof.
That follows from the Theorem
3.5
and (
3.31
). ∎

The next result shows that
the condition
\mathfrak{m}_{\bf s}(\mathbb{R}_{+})<\infty
guarantees that part of the wave propagates like a traveling wave in (
3.23
). In that theorem,
P_{\bf ac}
denotes the orthogonal projection to
H_{\bf ac}(\mathcal{S}_{M})
.

Proposition 3.10
.

In the case when
\mathfrak{m}_{\bf s}(\mathbb{R}_{+})<\infty
, we have

(3.32)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to+\infty}\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathfrak{m}_{\bf s},[t-a,t+a])}=0,

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to+\infty}\|u(\cdot,t)-G_{u_{0}}(\cdot-t)\|_{L^{2}[t-a,t+a]}=0,

for some
G_{u_{0}}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and all
a>0
.
Moreover,
\|G_{u_{0}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}>0
if and only if
P_{\bf ac}u_{0}\neq 0
. If
u_{0}
is not identically equal to zero and has compact support, then
P_{\bf ac}u_{0}\neq 0
.

Proof.
These results are contained in Theorem
3.5
, Theorem
3.7
, and (
3.31
).∎

The statements made in Example
1.11
in Introduction now follow.

Example
1.12
: strings made of two types of material.
For another example, let us consider a string

\mathfrak{m}=\rho\,d\xi
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
with no singular part whose density
\rho
takes two positive values:
a
and
b
. Specifically,

(3.33)

\rho(\tau)=\begin{cases}a,&\tau\in E,\\
b,&\tau\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\setminus E,\end{cases}

for some Lebesgue-measurable set
E\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{+}
. We interpret such strings as those made from two types of material. Despite the relative simplicity, the model when
\rho
takes only two positive values can have a nontrivial spectrum, e.g., a spectrum with gap structure if
\rho
is periodic (see also
[
15
]
for analysis of related problems on the graphs).

In Example
1.12
of Introduction, we claimed

Proposition 3.11
.

Suppose
a\neq b
. We have
\sigma\in\mathrm{Sz}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
if and only if either
|\{\xi\colon\rho(\xi)=a\}|<\infty
or
|\{\xi\colon\rho(\xi)=b\}|<\infty
.

Proof.
We will apply Theorem
1.10
with properly chosen
\{\eta_{n}\}
. Define
\eta_{n}
by

\eta_{n}=\int_{0}^{n}\sqrt{\rho(\xi)}\,d\xi.

Thus,
\xi_{n}=n,n=0,1,\ldots
. Since
\rho
takes values
a
and
b
, we always have condition

0<C_{1}<\eta_{n+1}-\eta_{n}<C_{2},\quad n=0,1,2,\ldots

satisfied. For each
n\geqslant 0
, we have

(3.34)

\rho(\tau)=\begin{cases}a,&\tau\in E_{n},\\
b,&\tau\in F_{n},\end{cases}

where
|E_{n}|=\delta_{n},|F_{n}|=1-\delta_{n}
, and
E_{n}\subseteq[n,n+1),F_{n}\subseteq[n,n+1)
. Then,

\displaystyle 2\int_{n}^{n+2}\rho(\tau)\,d\tau

\displaystyle=2a(\delta_{n}+\delta_{n+1})+2b(2-\delta_{n}-\delta_{n+1})

\displaystyle=4b+2(a-b)(\delta_{n}+\delta_{n+1})

and

\displaystyle\left(\int_{n}^{n+2}\sqrt{\rho(\tau)}\,d\tau\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=(2\sqrt{b}+(\sqrt{a}-\sqrt{b})(\delta_{n}+\delta_{n+1}))^{2}

\displaystyle=4b+4\sqrt{b}(\sqrt{a}-\sqrt{b})(\delta_{n}+\delta_{n+1})+(\sqrt{a}-\sqrt{b})^{2}(\delta_{n}+\delta_{n+1})^{2}.

If we denote

A_{n}=2\int_{n}^{n+2}\rho(\tau)\,d\tau-\left(\int_{n}^{n+2}\sqrt{\rho(\tau)}\,d\tau\right)^{2},\qquad n\geqslant 0,

then the straightforward calculation shows

\displaystyle A_{n}

\displaystyle=2(a-b)(\delta_{n}+\delta_{n+1})-(4\sqrt{b}(\sqrt{a}-\sqrt{b})(\delta_{n}+\delta_{n+1})+(\sqrt{a}-\sqrt{b})^{2}(\delta_{n}+\delta_{n+1})^{2})

\displaystyle=(\sqrt{a}-\sqrt{b})^{2}(2-\delta_{n}-\delta_{n+1})(\delta_{n}+\delta_{n+1}).

Then, the string satisfies conditions in the left-hand side of (
1.25
) if and only if either
a=b
or

(3.35)

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(2-\delta_{n}-\delta_{n+1})(\delta_{n}+\delta_{n+1})<\infty.

Next, if either

(3.36)

|E|=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}|E_{n}|=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\delta_{n}<\infty

or

(3.37)

|E^{c}|=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}|F_{n}|=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(1-\delta_{n})<\infty,

then (
3.35
) converges. Conversely,
the convergence of the series (
3.35
) implies that

(3.38)

\lim_{n\to\infty}(2-\delta_{n}-\delta_{n+1})(\delta_{n}+\delta_{n+1})=0.

Since
\delta_{n}\in[0,1]
for each
n
, one can not have
\delta_{l}+\delta_{l+1}<\varepsilon
and
2-(\delta_{k}+\delta_{k+1})<\varepsilon
simultaneously if
|k-l|=1
and
\varepsilon<\frac{1}{2}
. Hence, (
3.38
) gives that
either
\lim_{n\to\infty}\delta_{n}=0
or
\lim_{n\to\infty}(1-\delta_{n})=0
. In the former case, (
3.35
) is equivalent to (
3.36
) and in the latter case, it is equivalent to (
3.37
).∎

Remark 3.12
.

Taking
b=0
in (
3.34
), one gets a string made of one type of material which can be distributed with “gaps” over
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. The application of Theorem
1.10
with
\eta_{n}=n,n=0,1,\ldots
yields the similar result. Namely,
\sigma\in\mathrm{Sz}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
if and only if
|\{\xi\colon\rho(\xi)=0\}|<\infty
.

Indeed, in that case condition (
1.25
) reads (recall that
\xi_{n}=L_{M}(n)
and
F=\{\xi\colon\rho(\xi)=0\}
)

(3.39)

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\Big(2\sqrt{a}(\xi_{n+2}-\xi_{n})-4\Big)<\infty.

Since

n=\sqrt{a}\int_{0}^{\xi_{n}}\chi_{E}d\xi=\sqrt{a}(\xi_{n}-|[0,\xi_{n}]\cap F|),

one has

\sqrt{a}|[0,\xi_{n}]\cap F|=\sqrt{a}\xi_{n}-n

so the sum in (
3.39
) converges if and only if

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}|[\xi_{n},\xi_{n+2}]\cap F|<\infty.

Since
\lim_{n\to\infty}\xi_{n}=+\infty
, the last condition is equivalent to
|F|<\infty
. Notice that if
|F|=\infty
in the last example, adding singular measure
\mathfrak{m}_{\bf s}
can not place
\sigma
in
\mathrm{Sz}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
. Indeed, inserting
\mathfrak{m}_{\bf s}
does not change the grid
\{\xi_{n}\}
but it increases
M(\xi_{n+2})-M(\xi_{n})
in (
1.25
) making the total sum diverge.

4.
Dirac operators

We start this section by recalling the definition of the one-dimensional Dirac operator. Then, we make the connection to canonical systems and explain how the results from the second section can be applied to prove the theorems stated in Section
1.2
.

4.1.
Dirac operators

Recall that the one-dimensional Dirac operator
\mathfrak{D}_{Q}
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
is defined by

(4.1)

\mathfrak{D}_{Q}\colon Z\mapsto JZ^{\prime}+QZ,\qquad J=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right),\qquad Q=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}q_{1}&q_{2}\\
q_{2}&-q_{1}\end{smallmatrix}\right).

Here the functions
q_{1},q_{2}
are real and belong to
L^{1}_{\rm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
.
The “free” Dirac operator with potential
Q=0
will be denoted by
\mathfrak{D}_{0}
. The domain of
\mathfrak{D}_{Q}
is given by

\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\mathfrak{D}_{Q}=\left\{Z\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})\colon\;\begin{aligned} &Z\mbox{ is locally absolutely continuous on }\mathbb{R}_{+},\\
&JZ^{\prime}+QZ\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2}),\\
&\langle Z(0),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
1\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}=0.\end{aligned}\right\}.

With this domain, the operator
\mathfrak{D}_{Q}
is a densely defined self-adjoint operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})
, see Section 8.6 in
[
63
]
or
[
12
]
for recent developments. Let
\Psi
denote the generalized eigenvector of
\mathfrak{D}_{Q}
:

(4.2)

J\Psi^{\prime}(\tau,z)+Q\Psi(\tau,z)=z\Psi(\tau,z),\quad\Psi(0,z)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right),\qquad\tau\geqslant 0,\quad z\in\mathbb{C},

where the derivative is taken with respect to
\tau
. Then, there exists a unique Borel measure
\mu_{D}
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that the generalized Fourier transform

(4.3)

\mathcal{F}_{Q}\colon Z\mapsto\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\langle Z(\tau),\Psi(\tau,\bar{z})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}\,d\tau,\qquad z\in\mathbb{C},

densely defined on
L_{c}^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})
, can be extended to a unitary operator from
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})
to
L^{2}(\mu_{D})
. That measure is called the main spectral measure of
\mathfrak{D}_{Q}
.

One can see that
\mathfrak{D}_{0}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}
are the same operators acting on the same Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})
and giving rise to identical generalized eigenvectors, generalized Fourier transforms, and main spectral measures (cf. (
4.2
), (
4.3
) and (
2.5
), (
2.7
)).

4.2.
The reduction of Dirac operator to a canonical system and the Szegő condition

The following result is well-known, see, e.g., Section 2.4 in
[
9
]
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
Q\in L^{1}_{\rm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
be as in (
4.1
), and let the matrix-valued function
N_{0}
be the solution of the Cauchy problem

(4.4)

JN_{0}^{\prime}(\tau)+Q(\tau)N_{0}(\tau)=0,\quad\tau\geqslant 0,\quad N_{0}(0)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right).

Denote by
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{H}}
the self-adjoint operator on
H
corresponding to the canonical system generated by the Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}=N_{0}^{*}N_{0}
. Then, the main spectral measures of the operators
\mathfrak{D}_{Q}\colon L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})
and
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{H}}\colon H\to H
coincide. In particular, the operators
\mathfrak{D}_{Q}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{H}}
are unitary equivalent and the unitary equivalence is given by the operator
V\colon X\mapsto N_{0}X
, which is a unitary map from
H
to
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})
. Moreover, we have
\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}}=\mathcal{F}_{Q}V
.

An important property of the locally absolutely continuous Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}=N_{0}^{*}N_{0}
in the previous lemma is that it has unit determinant everywhere on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Indeed, the Wronskian in problem (
4.4
) is constant so
\det N_{0}(\tau)=\det N_{0}(0)=1
. Hence,

(4.5)

\det\mathcal{H}(\tau)=1,\quad T_{\mathcal{H}}(\tau)=\tau,\qquad L_{\mathcal{H}}(\eta)=\eta\qquad\tau,\eta\in\mathbb{R}_{+},

for the corresponding functions
T_{\mathcal{H}}
and
L_{\mathcal{H}}
introduced in (
2.18
).
The identity
\det N_{0}(\tau)=1
has other important implications. First, in the polar decomposition
N_{0}=O|N_{0}|

the matrix
|N_{0}|
satisfies

|N_{0}|=\sqrt{N_{0}^{*}N_{0}}=\mathcal{H}^{\frac{1}{2}},\quad\det|N_{0}|=\det\mathcal{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}=(\det\mathcal{H})^{\frac{1}{2}}=1

and the rotation matrix
O
is defined uniquely and is locally absolutely continuous.
Second, the space
H
coincides with
L^{2}(\mathcal{H})
defined in (
2.2
). Moreover, if function
X
is compactly supported, then
VX
is also compactly supported and their supports coincide.

The spectral measures
\mu_{D}
of the Dirac operators define a proper subset of all Poisson-finite measures on the real line that generate the canonical systems as discussed in the second section. Some of them belong to the Szegő class. Thanks to the characterization (
2.25
), we have the following proposition (see Corollary 1.4 in
[
7
]
):

Proposition 4.2
.

The condition
N_{0}^{*}N_{0}\in\mathbf{Sz}{\rm(CS)}
is necessary and sufficient for the spectral measure
\mu_{D}
of the Dirac operator to satisfy
\mu_{D}\in\mathrm{Sz}(\mathbb{R})
.

Checking that
N^{*}_{0}N_{0}\in\mathbf{Sz}{\rm(CS)}
is not always easy. However, in many cases, the application of our proposition is straightforward.

Proposition 4.3
.

Suppose that the potential
Q
with entries in
L^{1}_{\rm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
has the form

(4.6)

Q=\begin{pmatrix}q&0\\
0&-q\end{pmatrix}\quad\mbox{or}\quad Q=\begin{pmatrix}0&q\\
q&0\end{pmatrix}.

Then, for the spectral measure
\mu_{D}
of the corresponding Dirac operator
\mathfrak{D}_{Q}
we have

(4.7)

\mu_{D}\in\mathrm{Sz}(\mathbb{R})\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad N_{0}^{*}N_{0}\in\mathbf{Sz}{\rm(CS)}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\sum_{n\geqslant 0}\left(\int_{n}^{n+2}h(\tau)\,d\tau\int_{n}^{n+2}\frac{d\tau}{h(\tau)}-4\right)<\infty,

where
h(\tau)=e^{2\int_{0}^{\tau}q(s)\,ds}
,
\tau\geqslant n
.

Proof.
The first equivalence has already been discussed and we need to show the second one. For the potentials
Q
of the form (
4.6
), define
g(\tau)=\int_{0}^{\tau}q(s)\,ds
. Then, solving the problem (
4.4
) to find
N_{0}
is easy. That gives

N_{0}(\tau)=\begin{pmatrix}\cosh g(\tau)&\sinh g(\tau)\\
\sinh g(\tau)&\cosh g(\tau)\end{pmatrix}\quad\mbox{or}\quad N_{0}(\tau)=\begin{pmatrix}e^{-g(\tau)}&0\\
0&e^{g(\tau)}\end{pmatrix},

respectively. Therefore, for
\mathcal{H}=N_{0}^{*}N_{0}
, we have

\mathcal{H}(\tau)=\begin{pmatrix}\cosh 2g(\tau)&\sinh 2g(\tau)\\
\sinh 2g(\tau)&\cosh 2g(\tau)\end{pmatrix}\quad\quad\mbox{or}\quad\mathcal{H}(\tau)=\begin{pmatrix}e^{-2g(\tau)}&0\\
0&e^{2g(\tau)}\end{pmatrix}.

In both cases
\det\mathcal{H}=1
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, and the straightforward calculation yields

\det\int_{n}^{n+2}\mathcal{H}(\tau)\,d\tau=\int_{n}^{n+2}e^{2g(\tau)}\,d\tau\int_{n}^{n+2}e^{-2g(\tau)}\,d\tau=\int_{n}^{n+2}h(\tau)\,d\tau\int_{n}^{n+2}\frac{1}{h(\tau)}\,d\tau.

So,
\mathcal{H}\in\mathbf{Sz}{\rm(CS)}
if and only if (
4.7
) holds. The result follows. ∎

Remark 4.4
.

Propositions
4.2
and
4.3
imply Theorem
1.3
.

4.3.
The evolution for Dirac equation and Møller wave (modified wave) operators

The self-adjoint operator
\mathfrak{D}_{Q}
defines a unitary evolution
e^{it\mathfrak{D}_{Q}}
. Lemma
4.1
above gives the connection between
e^{it\mathfrak{D}_{Q}}
and evolution
e^{it\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{H}}}
for canonical systems.

Lemma 4.5
.

If
\mathcal{H}=N_{0}^{*}N_{0}
, then

e^{it\mathfrak{D}_{Q}}=Ve^{it\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{H}}}V^{-1}

for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

For every
Z
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})=L^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{0})
we again define the
front
as

\mathfrak{fr}[Z]=\inf\{\tau\geqslant 0\colon\;Z(s)=0\;\mbox{for almost every }s>\tau\}.

Notice that all elements of
N_{0}
are real-valued so
Z
has real components if and only if
X=V^{-1}Z
has real components.

Proposition 4.6
.

For every real
Z\in L_{c}^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})
and every
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\mathfrak{fr}[e^{it\mathfrak{D}_{Q}}Z]=|t|+\mathfrak{fr}[Z].

Proof.
That follows from Theorem
2.1
and formula (
4.5
).∎

Remark 4.7
.

For an arbitrary
Z\in L_{c}^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})
, we can write each of its components as a sum of real and imaginary parts. Then, Proposition
4.6
gives

\mathfrak{fr}[e^{it\mathfrak{D}_{Q}}Z]\leqslant|t|+\mathfrak{fr}[Z]
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

We will also need the following proposition.

Proposition 4.8
.

Let
\mu
be the spectral measure of the Dirac operator
\mathfrak{D}_{Q}
. Suppose
\mu\notin\mathrm{Sz}(\mathbb{R})
. Then, for every
Z\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})
, we have

\lim_{t\to\pm\infty}\|e^{it\mathfrak{D}_{Q}}Z\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{2},[|t|-b,|t|+b])}=0

for every
b>0
.

Proof.
That follows from Corollary
2.11
and formula (
4.5
).∎

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.
Take a nonzero function
Y\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})
. Set

Z_{Y,+}=W_{+}(\mathfrak{D}_{Q},\mathfrak{D}_{0},\gamma)Y=\lim_{t\to+\infty}e^{-it\mathfrak{D}_{Q}}M_{\gamma}e^{it\mathfrak{D}_{0}}Y,

and notice that
\|Z_{Y,+}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})}=\|Y\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})}>0
.
That yields

(4.8)

\lim_{t\to+\infty}\|M_{\gamma}e^{it\mathfrak{D}_{0}}Y-e^{it\mathfrak{D}_{Q}}Z_{Y,+}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})}=0.

Formula (
2.73
) for
Y=\begin{pmatrix}Y_{1}\\
Y_{2}\end{pmatrix}
can be recast as

(4.9)

e^{it\mathfrak{D}_{0}}\begin{pmatrix}Y_{1}\\
Y_{2}\end{pmatrix}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}Y_{1}(\tau-t)+Y_{1}(\tau+t)\\
-i(Y_{1}(\tau-t)-Y_{1}(\tau+t))\end{pmatrix}+\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}i(Y_{2}(\tau-t)-Y_{2}(\tau+t))\\
Y_{2}(\tau-t)+Y_{2}(\tau+t)\end{pmatrix},\qquad\tau\in\mathbb{R}_{+},

where
Y_{1}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
is extended to the whole real line
\mathbb{R}
as an even function and
Y_{2}
is extended as odd function.
That gives

\liminf_{t\to+\infty}\|e^{it\mathfrak{D}_{0}}Y\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{2},[t-b,t+b])}>0

for large enough
b
. From (
4.8
), one gets
\liminf_{t\to+\infty}\|e^{it\mathfrak{D}_{Q}}Z_{Y,+}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{2},[t-b,t+b])}>0
. Now we have
\mu\in\mathrm{Sz}(\mathbb{R})
by Proposition
4.8
. The case when
t\to-\infty
can be handled similarly. ∎

Remark 4.9
.

Notice that we have used the existence of
\lim_{t\to+\infty}e^{-it\mathfrak{D}_{Q}}M_{\gamma}e^{it\mathfrak{D}_{0}}Y
for just one nonzero element
Y\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})
in the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.
Assume that the main spectral measure
\mu_{D}
of
\mathfrak{D}_{Q}
is in the Szegő class. By Lemma
4.1
,
\mu_{D}
coincides with the spectral measure of the Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}=N_{0}^{*}N_{0}
generated by the solution of equation
JN_{0}^{\prime}+QN_{0}=0
,
N_{0}(0)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. Taking into account (
4.5
), Theorem
2.22
tells us that for some function
\gamma_{0}\colon\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{T}
and for every
Y\in L^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{0})=L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})
, the limits

\lim_{t\to+\infty}e^{-it\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{H}}}M_{\gamma_{0}}\mathcal{H}^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{it\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}}Y,\quad\lim_{t\to-\infty}e^{-it\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{H}}}M_{\overline{\gamma}_{0}}\mathcal{H}^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{it\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}}Y

exist in the norm of
L^{2}(\mathcal{H})
. Since
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}=\mathfrak{D}_{0}
, that implies existence of the limit

\lim_{t\to+\infty}Ve^{-it\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{H}}}M_{\gamma_{0}}\mathcal{H}^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{it\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}}Y=\lim_{t\to+\infty}e^{-it\mathfrak{D}_{Q}}VM_{\gamma_{0}}\mathcal{H}^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{it\mathfrak{D}_{0}}Y

in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})
.
Note that
(N_{0}(\tau)\mathcal{H}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\tau))^{*}(N_{0}(\tau)\mathcal{H}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\tau))
is the identity matrix for each
\tau\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Since

\det(N_{0}(\tau)\mathcal{H}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\tau))=1,\qquad\tau\in\mathbb{R}_{+},

it follows that the operator
Y\mapsto V\mathcal{H}^{-\frac{1}{2}}Y
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})
coincides with the multiplication operator by a
2\times 2
matrix-valued function of the form

\Sigma_{\varphi(\tau)}=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\varphi(\tau)&\sin\varphi(\tau)\\
-\sin\varphi(\tau)&\cos\varphi(\tau)\end{pmatrix},\qquad\varphi(\tau)\in[0,2\pi).

We have

\Sigma_{\varphi}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
-i\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\varphi-i\sin\varphi\\
-\sin\varphi-i\cos\varphi\end{pmatrix}=e^{-i\varphi}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
-i\end{pmatrix}.

Formula (
4.9
) shows that when
t\to+\infty
, for every
Y\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})
we have

e^{it\mathfrak{D}_{0}}\begin{pmatrix}Y_{1}\\
Y_{2}\end{pmatrix}=\frac{Y_{1}(\tau-t)}{2}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
-i\end{pmatrix}+\frac{iY_{2}(\tau-t)}{2}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
-i\end{pmatrix}+o(1),

where
o(1)
is with respect to
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})
–norm. Therefore,

e^{-it\mathfrak{D}_{Q}}VM_{\gamma_{0}}\mathcal{H}^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{it\mathfrak{D}_{0}}Y=e^{-it\mathfrak{D}_{Q}}M_{\gamma_{0}e^{-i\varphi}}e^{it\mathfrak{D}_{0}}Y+o(1),\qquad t\to+\infty

and the limit

W_{+}(\mathfrak{D}_{Q},\mathfrak{D}_{0},\gamma)Y=\lim_{t\to+\infty}e^{-it\mathfrak{D}_{Q}}M_{\gamma}e^{it\mathfrak{D}_{0}}Y

exists in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})
for all
Y\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})
if we take
\gamma=\gamma_{0}e^{-i\varphi}
. The existence of the wave operator
W_{+}(\mathfrak{D}_{Q},\mathfrak{D}_{0},\gamma)
follows. Arguing similarly, one can prove the existence of
W_{-}(\mathfrak{D}_{Q},\mathfrak{D}_{0},\gamma)
with the modification to the dynamics given by
M_{\overline{\gamma}}
. Moreover, the proof shows that

\mathop{\mathrm{Ran}}\nolimits W_{\pm}(\mathfrak{D}_{Q},\mathfrak{D}_{0},\gamma)=V(\mathop{\mathrm{Ran}}\nolimits W(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{H}},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{H}_{0}},\gamma))=\mathcal{F}_{Q}^{-1}\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}}(H_{\bf ac}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{H}}))=\mathcal{F}_{Q}^{-1}(L^{2}(\mu_{\bf ac}))=H_{\bf ac}(\mathfrak{D}_{Q}),

where
\mu_{\bf ac}=w\,dx
is the absolutely continuous part of the measure
\mu
and
\mu=\mu_{D}
. In other words, the wave operators
W_{\pm}(\mathfrak{D}_{Q},\mathfrak{D}_{0},\gamma)
are complete. It is also clear from the proof that our construction gives
\gamma=1
in the case where
Q
is anti-diagonal (
q_{1}=0
). ∎

Proof of Theorem
1.5
.
Given Lemma
4.5
, Theorem
1.5
is a direct consequence of Theorem
2.24
and Theorem
2.26
. ∎

Proof of Theorem
1.6
.
Given Lemma
4.5
, Theorem
1.6
follows from Theorem
2.24
. ∎

4.4.
Wiegner-von Neumann potentials

In this subsection, we prove Theorem
1.4
. Let us recall its statement for convenience:

Suppose that a potential
Q\in L^{1}_{\rm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
has the form (
4.6
), with
q=\frac{\sin\tau^{\alpha}}{\tau^{\beta}}

on
[\tau_{0},+\infty)
for some
\tau_{0}>0
and
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
. Then,
Q\in\mathbf{Sz}{\rm(Dir)}
if and only
(\alpha,\beta)\in A
, where

A=\{\alpha\leqslant 0,\;\beta-\alpha>\frac{1}{2}\}\cup\{\alpha\in(0,1),\;\beta>\frac{1}{2}\}\cup\{\alpha\geqslant 1,\;\alpha+\beta>\frac{3}{2}\}

is the open set depicted on Figure
1
.

We will need a variant of Korey’s estimate from
[
51
]
. Recall that we use notation
\langle f\rangle_{I}=\frac{1}{|I|}\int_{I}f(x)\,dx
.

Lemma 4.10
.

Suppose
I=[a,b]
and measurable function
f\colon I\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies

(4.10)

\langle e^{f}\rangle_{I}\cdot\langle e^{-f}\rangle_{I}=1+\varepsilon,\qquad\varepsilon\in[0,1].

Then,

(4.11)

\langle\left|f-\langle f\rangle_{I}\right|\rangle_{I}\leqslant c\sqrt{\varepsilon},

for a universal constant
c
.

Proof.
In
[
51
]
, formula (3.7), Korey shows that

\langle e^{f}\rangle_{I}\cdot\exp\left(-\langle f\rangle_{I}\right)=1+\varepsilon^{\prime},\qquad\varepsilon^{\prime}\in[0,1],

implies

\langle|f-m_{I}(f)|\rangle\leqslant C\sqrt{\varepsilon^{\prime}},

where
m_{I}(f)
denotes a median of
f
over
I
. By Jensen’s inequality,

\exp\left(-\langle f\rangle_{I}\right)\leqslant\langle e^{-f}\rangle_{I}.

Therefore, (
4.10
) implies

\langle|f-m_{I}(f)|\rangle\leqslant C\sqrt{\varepsilon}.

Now, for every
c^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\left|f-\langle f\rangle_{I}\right|\rangle_{I}\leqslant\langle\left|f-c^{\prime}\right|\rangle_{I}+|c^{\prime}-\langle f\rangle_{I}|\leqslant 2\langle\left|f-c^{\prime}\right|\rangle_{I}.

Taking
c^{\prime}=m_{I}(f)
finishes our proof.∎

For integer
n\geqslant 0
, we let
I_{n}=[n,n+2]
. Given real-valued
q\in L^{1}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
, denote
g_{n}(\tau)=2\int_{n}^{\tau}q(s)\,ds
.

Proposition 4.11
.

If
\lim_{n\to+\infty}\|g_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}(I_{n})}=0
, then

(4.12)

\sum_{n\geqslant 0}\left(\int_{I_{n}}e^{g_{n}}\,d\tau\int_{I_{n}}e^{-g_{n}}\,d\tau-4\right)<\infty\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\sum_{n\geqslant 0}\int_{I_{n}}|g_{n}-\langle g_{n}\rangle_{I_{n}}|^{2}\,d\tau<+\infty.

Proof.
Set
\widetilde{g}_{n}=g_{n}-\langle g_{n}\rangle_{I_{n}}
and notice that

\langle e^{g_{n}}\rangle_{I_{n}}\cdot\langle e^{-g_{n}}\rangle_{I_{n}}=\langle e^{\widetilde{g}_{n}}\rangle_{I_{n}}\cdot\langle e^{-\widetilde{g}_{n}}\rangle_{I_{n}}.

Then, since
\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\widetilde{g}_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}(I_{n})}=0
, we use Taylor expansion to get

\displaystyle\int_{I_{n}}e^{\pm\widetilde{g}_{n}}\,d\tau

\displaystyle=2\pm\int_{I_{n}}\widetilde{g}_{n}\,d\tau+\frac{1}{2}\int_{I_{n}}\widetilde{g}^{2}_{n}\,d\tau+O\left(\int_{I_{n}}|\widetilde{g}_{n}|^{3}\,d\tau\right),

as
n\to+\infty
. It follows that

\int_{I_{n}}e^{\widetilde{g}_{n}}\,d\tau\int_{I_{n}}e^{-\widetilde{g}_{n}}\,d\tau=4+2\int_{I_{n}}\widetilde{g}^{2}_{n}(\tau)\,d\tau+o\left(\int_{I_{n}}\widetilde{g}^{2}_{n}(\tau)\,d\tau\right),

which proves the required claim. ∎

Proof of Theorem
1.4
.
Recall Proposition
4.3
, Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
. To prove our result, we only need to establish the range of parameters
\alpha
and
\beta
for which the condition

(4.13)

\sum_{n\geqslant 0}\left(\int_{I_{n}}e^{g_{n}}\,d\tau\int_{I_{n}}e^{-g_{n}}\,d\tau-4\right)<\infty

is satisfied, where
g_{n}
is defined right before the Proposition
4.11
. For an integer
n\geqslant\max(1,\tau_{0})
and
\gamma=(\alpha+\beta-1)/\alpha
, we have

(4.14)

\frac{\alpha g_{n}(x)}{2}=\alpha\int_{n}^{x}\frac{\sin\tau^{\alpha}}{\tau^{\beta}}\,d\tau=\int_{n^{\alpha}}^{x^{\alpha}}\frac{\sin y}{y^{\gamma}}\,dy=\left.-\frac{\cos y}{y^{\gamma}}\right|_{n^{\alpha}}^{x^{\alpha}}-\gamma\left.\frac{\sin y}{y^{\gamma+1}}\right|_{n^{\alpha}}^{x^{\alpha}}-\gamma(\gamma+1)\int_{n^{\alpha}}^{x^{\alpha}}\frac{\sin y}{y^{\gamma+2}}\,dy.

Let us consider several cases.

Case
\alpha\geqslant 1
,
\alpha+\beta>1
.
In this case
\gamma>0
and we are in the setting of Proposition
4.11
. Note that

\sup_{x\in I_{n}}\left|-\gamma\left.\frac{\sin y}{y^{\gamma+1}}\right|_{n^{\alpha}}^{x^{\alpha}}-\gamma(\gamma+1)\int_{n^{\alpha}}^{x^{\alpha}}\frac{\sin y}{y^{\gamma+2}}\,dy\right|=O\left(\frac{1}{n^{\alpha(\gamma+1)}}\right)\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N}).

Thus, we only need to control the sum of dispersions

(4.15)

\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\Bigl\langle\left|\frac{\cos x^{\alpha}}{x^{\alpha\gamma}}-\Bigl\langle\frac{\cos x^{\alpha}}{x^{\alpha\gamma}}\Bigr\rangle_{I_{n}}\right|^{2}\Bigr\rangle_{I_{n}}=\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\left(\Bigl\langle\left|\frac{\cos x^{\alpha}}{x^{\alpha\gamma}}\right|^{2}\Bigr\rangle_{I_{n}}-\Bigl\langle\frac{\cos x^{\alpha}}{x^{\alpha\gamma}}\Bigr\rangle_{I_{n}}^{2}\right).

Set
\eta=\alpha\gamma+\alpha-1
, and note that
\eta\geqslant\gamma>0
. Similarly to (
4.14
), we have

\int_{I_{n}}\frac{\cos(x^{\alpha})}{x^{\alpha\gamma}}\,dx=\frac{1}{\alpha}\left(\frac{\sin(n+2)^{\alpha}}{(n+2)^{\eta}}-\frac{\sin n^{\alpha}}{n^{\eta}}\right)+O(n^{-\eta-\alpha})

and
n^{-\eta-\alpha}\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N})
. So, the question reduces to the convergence of the series

(4.16)

\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\left(2\alpha^{2}\int_{I_{n}}\frac{\cos^{2}x^{\alpha}}{x^{2\alpha\gamma}}\,dx-\left(\frac{\sin(n+2)^{\alpha}}{(n+2)^{\eta}}-\frac{\sin n^{\alpha}}{n^{\eta}}\right)^{2}\right).

Notice first that
\alpha\geqslant 1,\alpha+\beta>\frac{3}{2}
implies
2\alpha\gamma>1
and
2\eta>1
. We get convergence in that situation and (
4.13
) holds.

We claim that for
\alpha>1,1<\alpha+\beta\leqslant\frac{3}{2}
, the series diverges and (
4.13
) does not hold.
Indeed, in that case, one has

\int_{I_{n}}\frac{\cos^{2}x^{\alpha}}{x^{2\alpha\gamma}}\,dx\sim n^{-2\alpha\gamma},\quad n^{-2\eta}=o(n^{-2\alpha\gamma}),\quad n\to\infty,

and we get the claim because
2\alpha\gamma\leqslant 1
.

It is only left to consider
\alpha=1
and
0<\beta\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
. In that case,
\gamma=\beta
, and

\frac{\cos x}{x^{\gamma}}-\Bigl\langle\frac{\cos x}{x^{\gamma}}\Bigr\rangle_{I_{n}}=n^{-\beta}(\cos x-\langle\cos x\rangle_{I_{n}})+O(n^{-\beta-1}).

Since
\langle(\cos x-\langle\cos x\rangle_{I_{n}})^{2}\rangle_{I_{n}}\sim 1
and
\beta\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
, the series (
4.15
) diverges.

To summarize, if
\alpha\geqslant 1
and
\alpha+\beta>1
, then (
4.13
) holds if and only if
\alpha+\beta>\frac{3}{2}
.

Case
\alpha\geqslant 1
,
\alpha+\beta\leqslant 1
.
We will show that (
4.13
) fails for this range of parameters. Suppose, on the contrary, that (
4.13
) holds. Then, estimate (
4.11
) gives

(4.17)

\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\left(\int_{I_{n}}\left|g_{n}-\langle g_{n}\rangle_{I_{n}}\right|\,dx\right)^{2}<\infty.

Recall that
\gamma=(\alpha+\beta-1)/\alpha\leqslant 0
. Integration by parts gives

\frac{\alpha g_{n}(x)}{2}=\alpha\int_{n}^{x}\frac{\sin\tau^{\alpha}}{\tau^{\beta}}\,d\tau=n^{-\alpha\gamma}\cos n^{\alpha}-x^{-\alpha\gamma}\cos x^{\alpha}+O(n^{-\alpha(\gamma+1)}).

and

\frac{\alpha}{2}\langle g_{n}\rangle_{I_{n}}=n^{-\alpha\gamma}\cos n^{\alpha}+O(n^{-\alpha(\gamma+1)+1}).

Hence, uniformly in
x\in I_{n}
, we have

\frac{\alpha}{2}\left|g_{n}(x)-\langle g_{n}\rangle_{I_{n}}\right|=x^{-\alpha\gamma}|\cos x^{\alpha}|+O(n^{-\alpha(\gamma+1)+1}).

Since

\int_{I_{n}}x^{-\alpha\gamma}|\cos x^{\alpha}|\,dx+O(n^{-\alpha(\gamma+1)+1})\geqslant n^{-\gamma\alpha}\left(\int_{I_{n}}|\cos x^{\alpha}|\,dx+O(n^{-\alpha+1})\right)

and

(4.18)

\inf_{n}\int_{I_{n}}|\cos x^{\alpha}|\,dx\geqslant C>0

for every
\alpha\geqslant 1
, the terms in series (
4.17
) can be estimated from below by
cn^{-2\gamma\alpha}\geqslant c>0
for large
n
. So, (
4.13
) does not hold if
\alpha\geqslant 1
and
\alpha+\beta\leqslant 1
.

Case
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
\beta\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
.
We are going to show that (
4.13
) fails for this range of parameters. Assume, as before, that (
4.13
) holds, so that the sum in (
4.17
) is finite. Applying the mean-value theorem, we see that

\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\left(\int_{I_{n}}\left|g_{n}(x)-g_{n}(x_{n})\right|\,dx\right)^{2}<\infty

for some points
x_{n}\in I_{n}
. For each
n\geqslant 0
, one can choose an interval
\Delta_{n}\subset I_{n}
of length
\frac{1}{4}
such that
\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}\nolimits(x_{n},\Delta_{n})\geqslant\frac{1}{4}
. Then, again by the mean-value theorem (this time – on the interval
\Delta_{n}
), there exist points
\widetilde{x}_{n}\in\Delta_{n}
such that

\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\left|g_{n}(\widetilde{x}_{n})-g_{n}(x_{n})\right|^{2}\lesssim\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\left(\int_{I_{n}}\left|g_{n}(x)-g_{n}(x_{n})\right|\,dx\right)^{2}<\infty.

We see that

(4.19)

\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\left|\int_{x_{n}}^{\widetilde{x}_{n}}\frac{\sin\tau^{\alpha}}{\tau^{\beta}}\,d\tau\right|^{2}<\infty.

Since
\sin\tau^{\alpha}-\sin n^{\alpha}=O(n^{\alpha-1})
for
\tau\in I_{n}
, we have

\left|\int_{x_{n}}^{\widetilde{x}_{n}}\frac{\sin\tau^{\alpha}}{\tau^{\beta}}\,d\tau\right|\geqslant|\sin n^{\alpha}|\cdot J_{n}-O(n^{\alpha-1}J_{n}),\qquad J_{n}=\left|\int_{x_{n}}^{\widetilde{x}_{n}}\frac{d\tau}{\tau^{\beta}}\,\right|.

Given that
\beta\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
, we have

J_{n}\sim n^{-\beta}

because
x_{n}
and
\widetilde{x}_{n}
are at least
\frac{1}{4}
-distance apart.
The sequence
\{\frac{1}{2\pi}n^{\alpha}\}
is uniformly distributed mod
1
(see, e.g.,
Section I.3 in
[
57
]
). Therefore, there is
N_{0}
so that for every
N>N_{0}
,
we will have a bound

|\sin n^{\alpha}|>0.01

for at least
\frac{N}{2}
integer numbers
n\in[N,2N]
. Therefore, since
\alpha\in(0,1)
,

\sum_{n=N}^{2N}\Bigl||\sin n^{\alpha}|\cdot J_{n}-O(n^{\alpha-1}J_{n})\Bigr|^{2}\gtrsim N^{1-2\beta}.

Since
\beta\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
,
\lim_{N\to\infty}N^{1-2\beta}
is either equal to
1
or is infinite and we have a contradiction with (
4.19
). Therefore, (
4.13
) fails.

Case
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
\beta>\frac{1}{2}
.
We have

\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\int_{I_{n}}g_{n}^{2}\,dx\leqslant 4\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\int_{I_{n}}\left(\int_{n}^{x}\frac{d\tau}{\tau^{\beta}}\,\right)^{2}\,dx\leqslant 2\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\left(\int_{I_{n}}\frac{d\tau}{\tau^{\beta}}\,\right)^{2}\,dx\leqslant 8\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\frac{1}{n^{2\beta}}<\infty.

Hence, (
4.13
) is true for this range of parameters by Proposition
4.11
.

Case
\alpha\leqslant 0
,
\beta-\alpha>\frac{1}{2}
.
For this range of parameters, we have

\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\int_{I_{n}}g_{n}^{2}\,dx\lesssim\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\frac{1}{n^{2(\beta-\alpha)}}

due to the fact that
\sin y
is comparable to
y
when
y\in[0,1]
. Using Proposition
4.11
, we conclude that (
4.13
) holds if
\beta-\alpha>\frac{1}{2}
.

Case
\alpha\leqslant 0
,
\beta-\alpha\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
.
We claim that (
4.13
) fails in that situation. Arguing as in the case when
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
\beta\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
, we assume that (
4.13
) is true and obtain

(4.20)

\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\left|g_{n}(\widetilde{x}_{n})-g_{n}(x_{n})\right|^{2}<\infty,

for some points
x_{n},\widetilde{x}_{n}\in I_{n}
such that
|x-\widetilde{x}_{n}|>1/4
. Then,

\sum_{n\geqslant 0}n^{2(\alpha-\beta)}\lesssim\sum_{n\geqslant 0}\left(\int_{x_{n}}^{\widetilde{x}_{n}}\tau^{\alpha-\beta}\,d\tau\right)^{2}\leqslant\sum_{n\geqslant 0}\left|\int_{x_{n}}^{\widetilde{x}_{n}}\frac{\sin\tau^{\alpha}}{\tau^{\beta}}\,d\tau\right|^{2}<\infty

which leads to a contradiction. ∎

We end this section with one more example of an oscillating potential
Q\in\mathbf{Sz}{\rm(Dir)}
. As we mentioned in Section
1.2
of the Introduction, a simplest “physical” interpretation of the Dirac operator
\mathfrak{D}_{Q}
is that
it describes the one-dimensional particle of unit mass moving in a field defined by a potential
Q
of the form

(4.21)

Q=\begin{pmatrix}q_{1}&q_{2}\\
q_{2}&-q_{1}\end{pmatrix},\qquad q_{1}=\cos\left(2\int_{0}^{\tau}q\,ds\right),\qquad q_{2}=-\sin\left(2\int_{0}^{\tau}q\,ds\right),

where
q\colon\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
. It is natural to ask if there exist potentials
Q\in\mathbf{Sz}{\rm(Dir)}
that can be written that way. Since
q_{1}^{2}+q_{2}^{2}=1
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, we cannot have
Q\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
for any
p<\infty
. In particular, the standard summability test (see the discussion after Theorem
1.3
)

Q\in\bigcup_{p\in[1,2]}L^{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\Longrightarrow Q\in\mathbf{Sz}{\rm(Dir)}

does not give any example of such a potential
Q
. However, Proposition
4.11
can be used to construct such examples. Indeed, let us consider

q_{1}(\tau)=\cos\left(\frac{\pi}{2}(-1)^{[e^{\tau}]}\right)=0,\qquad q_{2}(\tau)=-\sin\left(\frac{\pi}{2}(-1)^{[e^{\tau}]}\right),\qquad\tau\in\mathbb{R}_{+},

where
[e^{\tau}]
stands for the integer part of
e^{\tau}
. Due to high oscillation of
q_{2}
, Proposition
4.11
applies and the corresponding potential
Q=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}q_{1}&q_{2}\\
q_{2}&-q_{1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&q_{2}\\
q_{2}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
belongs to
\mathbf{Sz}{\rm(Dir)}
.
Then, one can construct its small perturbation of the form (
4.21
) which belongs to
\mathbf{Sz}{\rm(Dir)}
(the Theorem
1.3
can be used for that approximation argument).

5.
Appendix

In this Appendix, we collect a few auxiliary results and prove some statements made in the main text.

5.1.
Proof of Proposition
2.8

The modification of the proofs in
[
7
]
yields the statement. Alternatively, one can argue as follows. First, we claim that (
2.25
) implies (
2.26
) for
\alpha_{n}=\lambda n
with any
\lambda>0
.
Indeed, if
m_{\lambda}
and
\mu_{\lambda}
denote Titchmarsh-Weyl function and spectral measure, respectively, of canonical system with Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}(\lambda\tau)
, then
m_{\lambda}(z)=m_{1}(\lambda^{-1}z)
as follows from (1-5) in
[
7
]
.
Now, it is enough to observe that
\mu_{\lambda}\in\mathrm{Sz}(\mathbb{R})\Longleftrightarrow\mu_{1}\in\mathrm{Sz}(\mathbb{R})
.

Second, we claim that, given intervals
I^{-}\subseteq I,|I|=1
, and
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
, the following implication holds

(5.1)

\det\int_{I}\mathcal{H}(\tau)d\tau-1=\varepsilon\Rightarrow\det\int_{I^{-}}\mathcal{H}(\tau)d\tau-|I^{-}|^{2}\lesssim\varepsilon,

for every non-negative Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}
that satisfies
\det\mathcal{H}=1
a.e. on
I
. Indeed, denote
A=\int_{I^{-}}\mathcal{H}d\tau
and
B=\int_{I^{+}}\mathcal{H}d\tau
where
I=I^{-}\cup I^{+}
. Then, we get (see, e.g., (A-1) in
[
7
]
):

\det A\geqslant|I^{-}|^{2},\quad\det B\geqslant|I^{+}|^{2}

and

\det(A+B)=1+\varepsilon.

Minkowski inequality for determinants yields

\det(A+B)\geqslant(\sqrt{\det A}+\sqrt{\det B})^{2}.

Denoting
\sqrt{\det A}=x
and
\sqrt{\det B}=y
, we get

x+y\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)^{\frac{1}{2}},\quad|I^{-}|+|I^{+}|=1,\quad|I^{-}|\leqslant x,\quad|I^{+}|\leqslant y.

That implies (draw the corresponding domains on the plane), that
|I^{-}|\leqslant x\leqslant|I^{-}|+C\varepsilon
. Taking the square of the last bound yields the estimate on the right-hand side of (
5.1
).

Now, if
\det\mathcal{H}=1
a.e., the statement in (
2.26
) holds by combining these two claims. Indeed, suppose
\mu\in\mathrm{Sz}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
and we are given sequence
\{\alpha_{n}\}
. Then, there is
\lambda
such that every interval
[\alpha_{n},\alpha_{n+2}]
is inside one of the intervals
[\lambda l,\lambda(l+2)]
or
[\lambda(l-1),\lambda(l+1)]
for some
l
. Since the sum in (
2.26
) converges for
\{\alpha_{l}\}=\{\lambda l\}
, we can apply (
5.1
) (with dilated and translated interval
I
) to get condition in the left-hand side of (
2.26
) satisfied for
\{\alpha_{n}\}
. Conversely, if the sum in (
2.26
) converges for some
\{\alpha_{n}\}
, then there is suitable
\lambda
such that each interval
[\lambda n,\lambda(n+2)]
is covered by either
[\alpha_{l},\alpha_{l+2}]
or
[\alpha_{l-1},\alpha_{l+1}]
for some
l
. Thus, applying (
5.1
) again, we get that the sum in (
2.26
) converges with
\{\alpha_{n}\}=\{\lambda n\}
and
\mu\in\mathrm{Sz}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
. The case of general
\mathcal{H}
follows by making the change of variables in
\tau
and using an approximation argument.∎

5.2.
Free evolution for canonical systems and Dirac operators.

Recall that
\mathfrak{D}_{0}=\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}
. We now show that the free evolution for these operators is, in fact, equivalent to the shift on the real line and that relation is algebraic. To this end, we work in terms of Dirac operator and perform two elementary unitary transformations

\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{0}=-Z^{-1}\mathfrak{D}_{0}Z=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-i\partial_{\tau}&0\\
0&i\partial_{\tau}\end{smallmatrix}\right),\quad Z=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}i&-1\\
1&-i\end{smallmatrix}\right).

Here,
\partial_{\tau}
stands for the differentiation operator. Operator
\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{0}
, taken with suitable boundary condition at
0
:
f_{1}(0)=if_{2}(0)
, is self-adjoint on
the same Hilbert space
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}f_{1}\\
f_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})
. If one further maps

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}f_{1}\\
f_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\mapsto g(x)=\begin{cases}f_{1}(x),&x>0,\\
if_{2}(-x),&x<0,\end{cases}

then,
\mathfrak{D}_{0}
becomes unitary equivalent to
-i\partial_{x}
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
with
e^{t\partial_{x}}g=g(x+t)
, which is the standard shift operator.

5.3.
A formula for exponential type

Lemma 5.1
.

If entire function
f
has bounded type both in
\mathbb{C}_{+}
and
\mathbb{C}_{-}
, then its exponential type can be computed by the formula

(5.2)

\mathop{type}\nolimits f=\limsup_{y\to+\infty}\frac{\log\max(|f(iy)|,|f(-iy)|)}{y}.

Proof.
Let us apply Theorem 2 in Lecture 16 of
[
62
]
. It says that for every entire function
f
of bounded type in
\mathbb{C}_{+}
and
\mathbb{C}_{-}
we have

\displaystyle\log|f(z)|

\displaystyle=\sigma_{+}y+o(|z|),\qquad y\geqslant 0,

\displaystyle\log|f(z)|

\displaystyle=\sigma_{-}y+o(|z|),\qquad y\leqslant 0,

outside of a set of disks
\{z\in\mathbb{C}\colon|z-a_{j}|<r_{j}\}
of finite view (the latter means that
\sum_{j}\frac{r_{j}}{|a_{j}|}<\infty
). Here
\sigma_{\pm}\in\mathbb{R}
and
y=\mathop{\rm Im}z
. Take
\varepsilon>0
and denote
\sigma=\max(\sigma_{+},-\sigma_{-})
. By the maximum principle for subharmonic functions, we have

\log|f(z)|\leqslant(\sigma+\varepsilon)|y|+o(|z|)

everywhere in
\mathbb{C}
as
z\to\infty
. Therefore, we have
\mathop{type}\nolimits f\leqslant\sigma
. On the other hand, the set of disks of finite view cannot fill any half-axis, hence

\sigma_{+}=\limsup_{y\to+\infty}\frac{\log|f(iy)|}{y},\qquad-\sigma_{-}=\limsup_{y\to-\infty}\frac{\log|f(iy)|}{|y|},

which proves the statement. ∎

5.4.
Rotation matrices

The following result in the linear algebra has been used in the main text.

Lemma 5.2
.

For every real
2\times 2
matrix
A
with non-negative determinant,
there is a rotation matrix
\Sigma_{\varphi}
of the form

\Sigma_{\varphi}=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\varphi&\sin\varphi\\
-\sin\varphi&\cos\varphi\end{pmatrix},\qquad\varphi\in[0,2\pi),

such that
\Sigma_{\varphi}A\geqslant 0
.

Proof.
This is immediate from the proof of the polar decomposition given in
[
32
]
, p. 276.
∎

5.5.
Robinson’s theorem

In the main text, we used the following variation of a result by Robinson
[
72
]
, which is based on ideas dating back to Ruelle’s work
[
74
]
.

Lemma 5.3
.

Suppose
H
is a Hilbert space,
\mathcal{D}
is a densely defined self-adjoint operator, and
P_{\Delta}
denotes the orthogonal projector for
\mathcal{D}
relative to a set
\Delta\subseteq\mathbb{R}
. If
A
is a bounded operator on
H
and
AP_{[-\Lambda,\Lambda]}
is compact for some
\Lambda\geqslant 0
, then

\lim_{\mathbf{T}\to+\infty}\frac{1}{\mathbf{T}}\int_{0}^{\mathbf{T}}\|Ae^{it\mathcal{D}}P_{[-\Lambda,\Lambda]}\psi\|^{2}dt=\sum_{j}\|AP_{E_{j}}P_{[-\Lambda,\Lambda]}\psi\|^{2}

for every
\psi\in H
, where
P_{E_{j}}
denotes the orthogonal projection on the eigenspace that corresponds to eigenvalue
E_{j}
and the sum is over all eigenvalues
\{E_{j}\}
of
\mathcal{D}
.

Proof.
The proof is an application of Theorem 2 in
[
72
]
to operator
\mathcal{D}P_{[-\Lambda,\Lambda]}
with the perturbation taken as
AP_{[-\Lambda,\Lambda]}
where both
\mathcal{D}P_{[-\Lambda,\Lambda]}
and
AP_{[-\Lambda,\Lambda]}
are considered as operators acting on
H
. ∎
